Volition Elicitation: Operational Semantics for People and
Their Machines (Full Version)
IDIT KEIDAR, Technion – Israel Institute of Technology, Israel
EHUD SHAPIRO, London School of Economics and Weizmann Institute of Science, UK and Israel
The most prevalent distributed systems today include people and their personal machines (smartphones), and
computations in them are driven by people’s volitions. Volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions were
proposed as an abstract specification language for such systems, in which a transaction has a precondition on
the machine states of its participants and is guarded by the volitions of some of them.
   Here we develop an abstraction cascade that derives smartphone apps from such specifications and prove it
correct down to the base programming language, GLP. The cascade consists of: (i) communicating volitional
agents (CVA), which implement every local volition-guarded specification, correctly, and completely for its
strongly fair runs, by two-phase commit coordinated by the initiating agent; (ii) volition-guarded GLP (vGLP),
an extension of GLP with volitional procedures, whose goals put an interactively-typed question to the person,
into which that implementation compiles; and (iii) GLP, which implements vGLP by eliciting the person’s
answers through the widgets of their interactive types, correctly, and completely for complete widgets. We
demonstrate the approach on three grassroots platforms — a social graph, a social network, and currencies: a
compiler carries each contract down the cascade to a GLP program that runs with scripted persons, taking the
words of its app from the contract too, and the apps of the three, so compiled, run on an iPhone simulator.
CCS Concepts: • Theory of computation → Operational semantics; • Software and its engineering →
Constraint and logic languages; Concurrent programming languages; Distributed programming languages; •
Human-centered computing → Human computer interaction (HCI); • Computer systems organization
→ Peer-to-peer architectures.
Additional Key Words and Phrases: Volition Elicitation, Operational Semantics, Concurrent Logic Programming,
GLP, Volitional Transactions, Grassroots Platforms, User Interface

1   INTRODUCTION
Volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions. The most prevalent distributed systems
today include people and their personal machines (smartphones). In such systems, computations are
driven by people’s volitions: a payment when a person wishes to pay someone, befriending when
two people wish to become friends, etc. Volition-Guarded Multiagent Atomic Transactions [28]
were proposed as an abstract specification language for such systems. In them (i) each agent
consists of a person and their machine; (ii) a multiagent atomic transaction has a precondition
on the machine states of its participants and is guarded by the volitions of some of them. For
example, the befriending of two people is guarded by both persons and unfriending by either; a
payment is guarded by the payer, a swap by both parties. An illustrative example is child-safe
online befriending [54]: it is guarded by the volitions of the two children and one parent of each,
and its precondition is that the machines do not record the two children as friends and do record
the two parents as friends (Appendix A.1).
Grassroots platforms. We focus on platforms that can have multiple, volitionally-forming in-
stances that may coalesce by mutual consent, possibly forming a single global instance, referred to
as grassroots platforms [28, 50, 52, 57]. Practically, a grassroots platform consists of people running
a person-to-person smartphone app.
Example: minting and swapping grassroots coins. Grassroots coins [49] are units of debt that
any person can issue (Section 7.3). The machine state of each agent is a multiset of atoms: ¢(𝑢)
in the state of Alice is a coin issued by 𝑢 and held by Alice, and knows(Bob) says that Alice holds
Bob’s public key. Two clauses of GC, the contract of grassroots coins, are “Alice may decide to issue
2                                                                                  Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


Alice-coins” and “Alice and Bob, who know each other, may jointly decide to swap coins they hold”.
Their speech-act schemas [16] are:

      GC: Speech-Act Schemas, clauses 1 and 2 [16]

    1. 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, Alice) ←
              guarded by Alice.
    2. 𝑘 1 · ¢(Alice, 𝑣), knows(Alice, 𝑣), ¬𝑘 · ¢(Alice, 𝑢), 𝑘 · ¢(Bob, 𝑢), knows(Bob, 𝑢), ¬𝑘 1 · ¢(Bob, 𝑣)
       ←
              knows(Alice, Bob), 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, 𝑢), knows(Bob, Alice), 𝑘 1 · ¢(Bob, 𝑣) guarded by Alice,
              Bob.

A schema is an implication, guarded by the persons who must will the act. Its head is what the act
adds and, negated, deletes, and its body what the act requires; the first argument of an atom is the
person who holds it, and 𝑘 · 𝜑 is 𝑘 copies of the atom 𝜑. Each schema denotes volition-guarded
transactions [28], one for each binding of its variables to agents and numbers.
Minting changes the state of one agent, and takes place only if its person wills it. A swap changes
the states of both parties at once, each giving coins and taking the other’s, and takes place only
if both persons will it. The currency agent of Section 5 implements both. A mint is a request of
its person. A proposed swap is put to the other party as an approval question, which shows the
proposal and has two buttons, yes and no.
Previous work. Previous work [28] presented volition-guarded specifications for the grassroots
social graph and grassroots currencies [49], and contracts written as speech-act schemas for these
two and for the grassroots social network [16, 51]. Volition-guarded transactions thus condition
machine transitions on persons’ volitions. Formally, such a transition can occur only if the will
for it is part of the volitional state of the person, namely is in the person’s head. Given today’s
technologies, the only way for a machine to know what’s in a person’s head is for the person
to express it; this is the fundamental reason for a UI. One of the contributions of this paper is to
rationalise the design of a UI that can elicit volitions from the person’s head, when needed. Existing
programming languages treat the person as a source of input — a stream of clicks [8, 10, 14, 25], an
event [4, 20, 40, 61], the environment [23, 62, 64] — so input drives computation, but in none does
a transaction wait on the wills of its guards, with an else-branch taken when a guard does not will
it (Section 9).
The abstraction cascade. Here we develop an abstraction cascade that derives smartphone grass-
roots apps from such abstract specifications, as shown in Figure 1, and prove it correct down to
the base programming language, GLP (Section 6.4). The passage from a legal text to a running
platform goes through the cascade, from the legal text at the top [16] to an app on each person’s
own phone at the bottom. This paper supplies the arrows (3)→(6), each proved correct, starting
from (3), volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions [28]. Its layers (4)–(8) in Figure 1 are:
(4) Communicating volitional agents. Implementing a multiagent atomic transactions-based
specification must go through unary transactions, carried out by each agent. In the case of such
volition-guarded transactions, the corresponding implementation-ready model is Communicating
Volitional Agents (CVA), defined in Section 3. Mathematically, CVA is but a restriction of the
general model, in which the only non-unary transactions are agent discovery, agent introduction
and message delivery. We prove that every CVA protocol is grassroots (Proposition 3.6). When
implementing CVA on a smartphone, the basic requirement from the smartphone UI is one: to elicit,
from the person operating the smartphone, the volitions the transactions require.

     (3) Volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions [28] (Section 2). Each agent a person
     and their machine; a transaction over several agents, with a precondition on their machine
     states and guarded by their persons’ volitions.

     (4) Communicating volitional agents (Section 3). An implementation-ready restriction of (3)
     in which the only non-unary transactions are agent discovery, agent introduction and message
     delivery.

     interactive types that determine the app’s user interface (Section 6).

Fig. 1. The abstraction cascade, from a legal text to a running platform. The framed (3)–(6) are this paper’s
subject: (3) is [28], recalled in Section 2, (4) is Section 3, and the arrows (3)→(6) are this paper. Each arrow
carries the reference in which its mapping is defined; the first, rendering a legal text as speech-act schemas,
is the one step left to judgement.



   A volition-guarded transaction changes the machine states of its participants at once, while a
communicating volitional agent changes only its own state and reaches another only by a message.
Here we implement the first by the second, for every local volition-guarded specification (Sec-
tion 3.2), correctly, and completely for its strongly fair runs. We use two-phase commit coordinated
by the initiating agent [19]. Wound-wait [45] orders contending attempts, and an attempt that
asks a person who does not will it closes by the else-branch of a volition-guarded if-then-else
transaction [28] (Section 2.2), taken when the person does not will its then-branch. The protocol is
free of deadlock and livelock. It is correct for runs in which an initiator knows its participants, no
agent is kept busy without end, a transaction that can be taken in several ways can, from some
point on, be taken in one of them, by the wills of the persons that way needs, and a machine that
can propose or request a transaction again and again does so again and again. It is complete for
the runs of the specification in which a transaction enabled again and again is taken again and
again. The model is asynchronous and fault-free: it has no notion of time, and no agent crashes or
misbehaves.
(5) Volition-guarded GLP. Volition-guarded GLP (vGLP) extends GLP with volitional procedures.
A goal of a volitional procedure puts a question to the person, and a clause that reads the answer
4                                                                                     Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


waits for it. A volitional procedure is declared procedure (𝑇 )*𝑝 (𝑇1, . . . ,𝑇𝑛 ): each goal 𝑝 (𝑆 1, . . . , 𝑆𝑛 )
of it receives an interactive variable of the interactive type 𝑇 as an added argument, and each clause
(𝐴)*𝑝 (𝑆 1, . . . , 𝑆𝑛 ) :- 𝐺 | 𝐵 matches it with its interactive term 𝐴. The interactive type says which
positions the program writes, for the person to see, and which positions the person writes, as their
answer.
   Two fragments of the currency agent (Section 7) preview the language, ... eliding arguments
a clause carries through unchanged. The first is the agent, whose question is the stream of the
person’s requests, served by an ordinary procedure, with the clause by which the person mints
coins. The second is a responder goal, spawned per arriving swap proposal, by which the person
answers it:
Request ::= mint(Amount)
          ; swap(Friend, GiveCoin, GiveAmount,
                 WantCoin, WantAmount)
          ; pay(Friend, Amount)
          ; redeem(Friend, WantCoin).
procedure (Stream(Request)?)*agent(...).

%% Ask the person for their requests, once, and serve them.
(Reqs)*agent(Id, ..., Outs, Holdings, Serial) :-
    serve(Reqs?, Id?, ..., Outs?, Holdings?, Serial?).

%% Mint_p(k), guarded by p: c'_p = c_p + cent^k_p.
serve([mint(Amount)|Reqs], Id, ..., Outs, Holdings,
      Serial) :-
    ground(Id?), integer(Amount?), Amount? >= 1 |
    create_coins(Id?, Amount?, Serial?, New),
    Serial1 := Serial? + Amount?,
    append_coins(Holdings?, New?, Holdings1),
    show(minted(Amount?), Outs?, Outs1),
    after_change(Reqs?, Id?, ..., Outs1?, Holdings1?,
                 Serial1?).

%% Show the person the offer, and pass their answer on.
Offer ::= offer(From, Want, Offered).
YesNo ::= yes ; no.
Approval ::= approval(Offer, YesNo?).
procedure (Approval)*respond_swap(Offer?, SwapResponse,
    Stream(UserInMsg)).
(approval(offer(From?, Want?, Offered?), Answer))*
  respond_swap(offer(From, Want, Offered), Resp?,
               Ans?) :-
    ground(From?), ground(Want?), ground(Offered?) |
    decide_swap(Answer?, From?, Want?, Offered?, Resp,
                Ans).
   The agent asks its question once, and the person answers it one request at a time. The mint
clause waits for a mint request at the head of the stream, which supplies Amount. The request form
stays on screen. The responder writes into its approval question the friend who proposes the swap,
Volition Elicitation                                                                                5


the coins they want and the coins they offer, and passes the person’s yes or no to decide_swap
(Section 5). The question has two buttons, and no is the decline.
The operational semantics of volition-guarded GLP. We then define the operational semantics
of vGLP. The state of each agent has two parts: the questions its person holds, and the goals its
machine is running, called its resolvent. A goal of a volitional procedure is first asked: the goal
receives one end of its interactive variable, and the person the other. A question reaches the person
with the program’s output in it written whole, and a clause that reads the person’s part reduces
only after the person has answered. An answer cannot be withdrawn. The machine cannot compel
the person, so in vGLP an answer carries no liveness obligation. We prove that on programs with
no volitional procedures the semantics coincides with GLP’s (conservativity), and that a question is
assigned only by its person’s answer (volitional soundness).
   We compile the canonical CVA platform of every effective local specification into vGLP, one goal
per agent (Section 5.4), and prove the compilation correct and its protocol grassroots.
(6) Grassroots Logic Programs and (7) Dart. For AI to derive smartphone-based grassroots
platforms from such abstract specifications, Grassroots Logic Programs (GLP) [53, 58, 59], a high-
level, polymorphically-typed, multiagent, concurrent logic programming language, was designed
and implemented in Dart/Flutter, which is deployable on most smartphones (iPhone/Android). So
far, GLP implementations of these platforms were derived in the same way from (volition-free)
multiagent atomic transactions [57].
   However, specifying the role of people in such platforms is out-of-reach for this formalism, and
hence the step of designing and implementing the app’s UI was an ad hoc, “manual” process.
   To rectify that, we need formal foundations for the implementation of volition-guarded transac-
tions, not only for attaining mathematical elegance and completeness, but, more pragmatically, for
AI to methodically develop the volition-eliciting UI for abstractly-specified grassroots platforms.
Moreover, we wish for a single smartphone app, running the GLP engine, to support multiple,
dynamically-loaded grassroots platforms written in GLP, and for that we cannot have an ad-hoc UI
for each new platform.
   As the semantics of vGLP requires the person’s answer before a reduction that reads it, a complete
implementation of vGLP in GLP must elicit it: finding out “what’s in the person’s head” is the
rationale for the UI. We derive the UI from the semantics: an open question is presented as the
widget of its interactive type, which shows what the program writes for the person and forms the
person’s input into a term of the type. Widgets are associated with types by declarations, scoped as
type declarations are, and every interactive type has a default widget built from its constructors.
We prove that a construct offers only answers of its question’s type, and every such answer where
its widget is complete (elicitation completeness). The implementation compiles vGLP onto GLP,
and we prove the implementation of vGLP by GLP correct, and complete for complete widgets.
(8) The app. We demonstrate the approach on the grassroots social graph, grassroots social network,
and grassroots currencies, starting from their contracts, written as speech-act schemas, and going
down the cascade: the CVA implementation of each platform is its canonical platform, and its vGLP
program is the compilation of that platform. A compiler takes a contract written as speech-act
schemas [16], whose roles are party roles or listed roles, to the vGLP compilation of its canonical
platform in one pass, producing neither the volition-guarded transactions the contract specifies
nor the platform. It compiles the social graph and the social network, GSG and GSN [16], and the
currencies, GC [49], and the compiled programs run on GLP with scripted persons. The apps of the
three, and of the base contract GKK they extend, so compiled, run as mini-apps of the grassroots
super-app [13] on an iPhone simulator (Section 8). Beside its clauses and its schemas a contract
6                                                                             Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


carries the words its app shows, which the compiler takes into the platform, so the app is compiled
from the contract.
Paper outline. Section 2 recalls volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions, volition-guarded
if-then-else transactions, transition systems and implementations; grassroots protocols are recalled
in Appendix A. Section 3 defines CVA, proves every CVA protocol volitionally grassroots, and
implements every local volition-guarded specification by CVA, by two-phase commit coordinated
by the initiating agent. Section 4 recalls GLP: its syntax, its reduction and its multiagent semantics.
Section 5 defines vGLP as its extension and the operational semantics vmaGLP, with volitional
soundness, then compiles the canonical CVA platform of every effective local specification into vGLP,
and proves the compilation correct. Section 6 derives the elicitation constructs from interactive types,
presents widgets and their declarations, proves elicitation completeness, defines the compilation
of vGLP onto GLP, and proves the implementation of vGLP by GLP correct, and complete for
complete widgets; it ends by composing the implementations of the cascade, from an effective
local specification to GLP. Section 7 presents the three platforms as the contracts the compiler
compiles, whose CVA implementations are their canonical platforms and whose vGLP programs
are the compilations of those platforms. Section 8 shows the apps of the three platforms and of
GKK, compiled down the cascade, on an iPhone simulator. Section 9 reviews related work, and
Section 10 concludes.

2 VOLITION-GUARDED MULTIAGENT ATOMIC TRANSACTIONS
This section recalls the mathematical foundations on which the paper is based [28, 48]. Each agent
consists of a person and a machine (e.g., smartphone) operated by the person. A machine transaction
specifies what machines do; a volition guard specifies which persons must be willing for it to occur.
   We assume a potentially infinite set of agents Π, but consider only finite subsets of it, so we
assume any particular set of agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π to be nonempty and finite. We use ⊂ to denote the strict
subset relation and ⊆ when equality is also possible. As standard, we use 𝑆 𝑃 to denote the set of all
total functions from 𝑃 to 𝑆, and if 𝑐 ∈ 𝑆 𝑃 we use 𝑐 𝑝 to denote the value of 𝑐 at 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃.

2.1   Volition-Guarded Transactions
   Definition 2.1 (Machine State, Configuration, Transaction, Volition-Guarded Multiagent Atomic
Transaction). Given an arbitrary set 𝑆 of machine states, with a designated initial state 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆, and
agents 𝑄 ⊂ Π, a machine configuration over 𝑄 is a member of 𝑆 𝑄 , and a machine transaction over
participants 𝑄 is a pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ ∈ (𝑆 𝑄 ) 2 such that 𝑐 ≠ 𝑐 ′ . Given such a machine transaction 𝑡, a
volition-guarded multiagent atomic transaction over 𝑡 — henceforth, volition-guarded transaction —
is a pair (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) where 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 are its guards.
   Here a guard is a person: the guards of a transaction are the participants who must will it, and a
transaction with no guards is unguarded. The condition on the machine states, that the participants
be in the states 𝑐 of 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ , its source, is the transaction’s precondition. Machine transactions are
atomic and asynchronous [48]—they can be carried out by their participants at any time, regardless
of the states of non-participants. Participants include both active agents (whose state changes) and
stationary agents (whose state is a precondition but does not change). Volition-guarded transactions
are machine transactions that can be carried out only if their guards 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 are willing. When we
say a transaction is “guarded by {𝑝, 𝑞},” both must be willing; when we say it is “guarded by either
𝑝 or 𝑞,” we mean there are two volition-guarded transactions over the same machine transaction,
(𝑡, {𝑝}) and (𝑡, {𝑞}), so that either person’s volition suffices. Distinct machine transactions can
represent “the same action” in different configurations, and we identify them by an equivalence
relation on machine transactions.
Volition Elicitation                                                                                         7


  Definition 2.2 (Transaction Equivalence). Given a set of machine transactions 𝑅, a transaction
equivalence is an equivalence relation ∼ on 𝑅 such that 𝑡 ∼ 𝑡 ′ implies 𝑡 and 𝑡 ′ have the same
participants, and the set 𝑅/∼ of its classes is countable. We write [𝑡] for the equivalence class of 𝑡
under ∼.
  A person’s volitional state is a set of equivalence classes of machine transactions they are willing
their machine to participate in. The will names the equivalence class, not a label on a transition:
one action is a set of transitions, one per configuration it may occur in, and the will is fulfilled by
whichever of them is taken.
   Definition 2.3 (Agent State and Configuration). Given agents 𝑃, states 𝑆 with initial state 𝑠 0 , a set
of machine transactions 𝑇 each over its own participants 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 and 𝑆, and equivalence ∼ on 𝑇 , an
agent state is a pair (𝑉 , 𝑚) ∈ A = (2𝑇 /∼ × 𝑆) where 𝑉 is its volitional state and 𝑚 ∈ 𝑆 its machine
state. The initial agent state is (∅, 𝑠 0 ). An agent configuration 𝑐 over 𝑃, 𝑆, 𝑇 , and ∼ is a member
𝑐 ∈ A 𝑃 in which 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 ⊆ (𝑇 /∼)𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, where (𝑇 /∼)𝑝 denotes the classes in 𝑇 /∼ in which
𝑝 is a participant; we write 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 for the volitional state and 𝑐𝑚
                                                                𝑝 for the machine state of agent 𝑝 in 𝑐.

  Definition 2.4 (Volitional Transaction [28]). Given agents 𝑃, states 𝑆, machine transactions 𝑇 over
𝑃 and 𝑆, and equivalence ∼ on 𝑇 :
(1) A volition change of agent 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 is a pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ of agent configurations over {𝑝}, 𝑆, 𝑇 , and ∼
    such that 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 , 𝑐 ′𝑣𝑝 ⊆ (𝑇 /∼)𝑝 and 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 ≠ 𝑐 ′𝑣𝑝 , and 𝑐𝑚   ′𝑚
                                                             𝑝 =𝑐 𝑝.
(2) A volitional machine transaction induced by a volition-guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ), for some
    𝑡 = (𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ ) ∈ 𝑇 over 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃, is a pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ where 𝑐 ≠ 𝑐 ′ are agent configurations
    over 𝑃, 𝑆, 𝑇 , and ∼ such that [𝑡] ∈ 𝑐𝑞𝑣 for every 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄 ′ ; 𝑐𝑚              ′𝑚    ′
                                                                     𝑝 = 𝑑𝑝 and 𝑐 𝑝 = 𝑑𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄;
           ′𝑚                                  ′𝑣
    𝑐 𝑝 = 𝑐 𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 \ 𝑄; and 𝑐 𝑝 = 𝑐 𝑝 \ {[𝑡]} for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃.
      𝑚                                                  𝑣

(3) A volitional transaction is a volition change or a volitional machine transaction.
  When a volitional machine transaction induced by (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) is taken, the class [𝑡] is removed from
every agent’s volitional state: the will is fulfilled by any equivalent transaction. A person may
independently change their volitional state via volition changes, which may add or remove classes;
beyond these, the framework removes a class from 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 only upon fulfilment.

2.2    Volition-Guarded If-Then-Else Transactions
In the transactions above, a person’s not willing a transaction has no consequence: no transaction
is enabled by the absence of a will. Communicating volitional agents (Section 3) need such a
consequence. Without it, the implementation of the transactions by communicating volitional
agents (Section 3.2) could not close an attempt whose guard does not will it, nor release the parts
its participants hold prepared for it. This is the volition-guarded if-then-else transaction of [28]: in
machine state 𝑠, if 𝑝 wills it, 𝑠 ′ is taken, else 𝑠 ′′ . Its else-branch is taken when the person does not
will the then-branch. In this paper the else-branch is used only within CVA.
   Definition 2.5 (Volition-Guarded If-Then-Else Transaction, Then-Branch, Else-Branch [28]). Given
machine states 𝑆, a volition-guarded if-then-else transaction over a single participant 𝑝 ∈ Π is a
triple 𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ ; 𝑠 ′′ guarded by 𝑝, with 𝑠, 𝑠 ′, 𝑠 ′′ ∈ 𝑆, 𝑠 ≠ 𝑠 ′ and 𝑠 ≠ 𝑠 ′′ ; the unary machine transaction
𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ is its then-branch and 𝑠 → 𝑠 ′′ its else-branch.
   Definition 2.6 (Else Transaction [28]). Given agents 𝑃, states 𝑆, machine transactions 𝑇 over 𝑃 and
𝑆, and equivalence ∼ on 𝑇 , an else transaction induced by a volition-guarded if-then-else transaction
𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ ; 𝑠 ′′ guarded by 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, with 𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ ∈ 𝑇 , is a pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ of agent configurations over 𝑃, 𝑆, 𝑇 ,
and ∼ such that [𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ ] ∉ 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 ; 𝑐𝑚          ′𝑚       ′′ 𝑚     ′𝑚                               ′𝑣
                                     𝑝 = 𝑠 and 𝑐 𝑝 = 𝑠 ; 𝑐 𝑞 = 𝑐 𝑞 for every 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 \ {𝑝}; and 𝑐 𝑞 = 𝑐 𝑞
                                                                                                          𝑣

for every 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃.
8                                                                                  Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


   The then-branch, taken when 𝑝 wills it, is the unary volition-guarded transaction (𝑠 → 𝑠 ′, {𝑝})
and induces volitional machine transactions per Definition 2.4; a unary volition-guarded transaction
is the special case with no else-branch, and an unguarded one is (𝑠 → 𝑠 ′, ∅). The else transaction is
taken when 𝑝 does not will the then-branch; it fulfils no will and removes no class. The two are
placed in one class below, so that liveness obliges the machine to take one branch or the other,
with no time bound. Only a unary guarded transaction has an else-branch: an unguarded one
requires no will, and a binary one would let an agent of another group, who never wills it, trigger
it (Definition A.3, Proposition A.6). Where a set of transactions may include volition-guarded
if-then-else transactions, as a CVA platform’s may (Section 3), volition-guarded transaction covers
them.

2.3   Transition Systems
   Definition 2.7 (Transition System, Computation, Run, Safe, Live, Correct [28, 48]). A transition
system is a tuple 𝑇 𝑆 = (𝐶, 𝑐 0,𝑇 , ∼) where 𝐶 is an arbitrary set of configurations, 𝑐 0 ∈ 𝐶 a designated
initial configuration, 𝑇 ⊆ 𝐶 ×𝐶 a set of transitions, each a pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ of non-identical configurations
𝑐 ≠ 𝑐 ′ ∈ 𝐶, and ∼ a partial equivalence relation on 𝑇 — an equivalence on a subset dom(∼) ⊆ 𝑇 ; we
write [𝑡] for the class of 𝑡 ∈ dom(∼) under ∼.
   A computation is a (nonempty, finite or infinite) sequence of configurations 𝑐 1, 𝑐 2, . . .; it is a run
                                                                                                        ∗
if 𝑐 1 = 𝑐 0 , and safe if 𝑐𝑖 → 𝑐𝑖+1 ∈ 𝑇 for every two consecutive configurations. We write 𝑐 →        − 𝑐′
for the existence of a safe computation from 𝑐 to 𝑐 ′ (empty if 𝑐 = 𝑐 ′ ). A class [𝑡] is enabled in 𝑐 if
𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ ∈ [𝑡] for some 𝑐 ′ . A run is live if no class [𝑡], 𝑡 ∈ dom(∼), is enabled in every configuration
of some suffix in which no member of [𝑡] occurs, and correct if it is safe and live.
   Liveness is weak fairness, per class. A partial equivalence, rather than a total one, is used so
that some transitions may carry no liveness requirement: only transitions in the domain of ∼ form
classes and thereby incur a liveness obligation, while transitions outside the domain — belonging
to no class — may occur in a correct run but are never required to.
   Definition 2.8 (Multiagent Transition System [48]). Given agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π and an arbitrary set 𝑆 of
local states with a designated initial local state 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆, a multiagent transition system over 𝑃 and 𝑆 is
a transition system 𝑇 𝑆 = (𝐶, 𝑐 0,𝑇 , ∼) with configurations 𝐶 := 𝑆 𝑃 , initial configuration 𝑐 0 := {𝑠 0 }𝑃 ,
transitions 𝑇 ⊆ 𝐶 2 a set of transactions over 𝑃 and 𝑆, and ∼ a partial equivalence on 𝑇 .
   Rather than specifying a multiagent transition system directly, we specify it via transactions,
each over its own participants; the closure operator induces from them transitions over a fixed 𝑃,
in which non-participants are stationary.
    Definition 2.9 (Transaction Closure [28]). Let 𝑃 ⊂ Π, 𝑆 a set of local states, and 𝐶 := 𝑆 𝑃 . For any
transition or transaction 𝑡 = 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ , we write 𝑡𝑞 := 𝑐𝑞 → 𝑐𝑞′ and say 𝑝 is stationary in 𝑡 if 𝑐 𝑝 = 𝑐 𝑝′ .
For a transaction 𝑡 = (𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ ) over 𝑆 with participants 𝑄, the 𝑃-closure of 𝑡, 𝑡↑𝑃, is the set of
transitions over 𝑃 and 𝑆 defined by:
                  (
                    {𝑡 ′ ∈ 𝐶 2 : ∀𝑞 ∈ 𝑄.(𝑡𝑞 = 𝑡𝑞′ ) ∧ ∀𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 \ 𝑄.(𝑝 is stationary in 𝑡 ′ )} if 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃
          𝑡↑𝑃 :=
                    ∅                                                                       otherwise
                                                                                                    Ð
If 𝑅 is a set of transactions, each 𝑡 ∈ 𝑅 over some 𝑄 and 𝑆, then the 𝑃-closure of 𝑅 is 𝑅↑𝑃 := 𝑡 ∈𝑅 𝑡↑𝑃.
Given a relation ∼ on 𝑅, its 𝑃-closure ∼↑𝑃 is the relation on 𝑅↑𝑃 with 𝑡ˆ (∼↑𝑃) 𝑡ˆ′ iff 𝑡ˆ ∈ 𝑡↑𝑃 and
𝑡ˆ′ ∈ 𝑡 ′ ↑𝑃 for some 𝑡 ∼ 𝑡 ′ .
  A transaction names only its participants. Its closure makes it transitions of the whole system
over 𝑃: the participants move as the transaction says, and every other agent stays where it is. The
Volition Elicitation                                                                                  9


closure and the equivalence do different jobs: the closure turns transactions into transitions, and
the equivalence groups the transitions that are one act. If the 𝑃-closures of distinct transactions
in 𝑅 are disjoint, then each transition in 𝑅↑𝑃 is induced by one transaction, and ∼↑𝑃 relates two
transitions exactly when their inducing transactions are related, so ∼↑𝑃 is a partial equivalence
whenever ∼ is. Disjointness holds when every participant of every transaction changes state, but a
participant need not: a participant whose state is part of the precondition and does not change,
such as a voter, is allowed, provided no other transaction in 𝑅 moves the same agents in the same
way. For volition-guarded transactions it is required only of unguarded ones (Definition 2.11).
  Definition 2.10 (Transactions-Based Multiagent Transition System, adapted from [28, 48]). Given
agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π, local states 𝑆 with initial local state 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆, a set of transactions 𝑅, each 𝑡 ∈ 𝑅
over some 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 and 𝑆, the 𝑃-closures of distinct transactions in 𝑅 being disjoint, and a partial
equivalence ∼ on 𝑅, the transactions-based multiagent transition system over 𝑃, 𝑆, 𝑅, and ∼ is the
multiagent transition system 𝑇 𝑆 = (𝑆 𝑃 , {𝑠 0 }𝑃 , 𝑅↑𝑃, ∼↑𝑃).
   A set of volition-guarded transactions with an equivalence on their underlying machine transac-
tions likewise induces a multiagent transition system, over agent states:
   Definition 2.11 (Volition-Guarded Transaction Set [28]). Let 𝑃 ⊂ Π be agents and 𝑆 machine
states with initial state 𝑠 0 . A volition-guarded transaction set over 𝑃 and 𝑆 is a set 𝑅 of volition-
guarded transactions and volition-guarded if-then-else transactions, where a volition-guarded
if-then-else transaction 𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ ; 𝑠 ′′ guarded by 𝑝 stands in 𝑅 for its then-branch (𝑠 → 𝑠 ′, {𝑝})
together with its else-branch, such that every (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅 has the participants of 𝑡 in 𝑃, and
every guard 𝑝 of a volition-guarded if-then-else transaction is in 𝑃. An equivalence ∼ on the set
𝑇𝑅 := {𝑡 : (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅 for some 𝑄 ′ } of underlying machine transactions (a total equivalence, hence
a partial equivalence in the sense of Definition 2.7) is compatible with 𝑅 if no else-branch is in
𝑇𝑅 , two volition-guarded if-then-else transactions with the same else-branch have ∼-equivalent
then-branches, and the 𝑃-closures of two machine transactions of different classes, each the machine
transaction of an unguarded transaction in 𝑅 or an else-branch, are disjoint.
  Definition 2.12 (Volitional Multiagent Transition System [28]). Given a volition-guarded transaction
set 𝑅 over 𝑃 and 𝑆 and an equivalence ∼ compatible with it, the volitional multiagent transition
system induced by (𝑆, 𝑅, ∼) over 𝑃 is the multiagent transition system (A 𝑃 , 𝑐 0,𝑇𝑉 , ∼𝑉 ) where:
(1) A := 2𝑇𝑅 /∼ × 𝑆 is the agent state space;
(2) 𝑐 0 ∈ A 𝑃 is the initial agent configuration, with 𝑐 0 𝑝𝑣 = ∅ and 𝑐 0𝑚
                                                                         𝑝 = 𝑠 0 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃;
                                             ′
(3) 𝑇𝑉 consists of all transitions 𝑒 → 𝑒 ∈ (A ) of one of three forms: (i) a volition change of
                                                    𝑃 2

    some 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃—𝑒𝑝𝑣 , 𝑒 ′𝑣𝑝 ⊆ (𝑇𝑅 /∼)𝑝 and 𝑒𝑝𝑣 ≠ 𝑒 ′𝑣𝑝 , 𝑒𝑝𝑚 = 𝑒 ′𝑚                 ′
                                                                  𝑝 , and 𝑒𝑟 = 𝑒𝑟 for every 𝑟 ∈ 𝑃 \ {𝑝};
    (ii) a volitional machine transaction induced by some volition-guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅
    per Definition 2.4(2); or (iii) an else transaction induced by some volition-guarded if-then-else
    transaction in 𝑅 per Definition 2.6;
(4) ∼𝑉 is the restriction of ∼↑𝑃 (Definition 2.9) to the volitional machine transactions and else trans-
    actions, relating two of them whenever their inducing machine transactions are ∼-equivalent,
    where the inducing machine transaction of an else transaction is the then-branch 𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ of its
    volition-guarded if-then-else transaction, and leaves every volition-change transition outside
    its domain.
  Every transition of 𝑇𝑉 other than a volition change has ∼-equivalent inducing machine trans-
actions, so ∼𝑉 is a partial equivalence. A step induced by a guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ), 𝑄 ′ ≠ ∅,
removes [𝑡] from the volitional state of each guard, which held it; a step of another class removes
another class, and an else transaction removes none, so neither is that step, and the 𝑃-closures of
10                                                                                           Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


two guarded transactions of different classes need not be disjoint. Two unguarded transactions of
different classes share no step, by the condition on 𝑃-closures. The two conditions on else-branches
give each else transaction one class: an else transaction is related through the then-branch of its if-
then-else transaction, and two if-then-else transactions with the same else-branch have equivalent
then-branches. The set 𝑅 of volition-guarded transactions that specifies a platform thus determines
the volitional machine transitions of the induced volitional multiagent transition system, while
volition-change transitions are freely available to every agent independent of 𝑅.
   We define when a volition-guarded transaction is enabled, combining the machine precondition
with the volitions of its guards.
   Definition 2.13 (Enabled [28]). Given a set of volition-guarded transactions, each (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) with
𝑡 = 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ a machine transaction over some 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 and 𝑆, and an equivalence ∼ on machine
transactions: the volition-guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) is machine-enabled in agent configuration
                                                                                              ′
             𝑝 = 𝑑𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄, and enabled if in addition [𝑡] ∈ 𝑐 𝑞 for every 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄 . The

transaction (𝑡 , 𝑄 ) with 𝑡 ∈ [𝑡] is enabled in 𝑐, or the else-branch of some volition-guarded

   A volition-guarded transaction with an empty guard (𝑄 ′ = ∅) requires no volitions and is enabled
whenever its machine precondition is met. The class of the then-branch of a volition-guarded
if-then-else transaction guarded by 𝑝 is thus enabled whenever 𝑝’s machine is in the state 𝑠: by
its then-branch if 𝑝 wills it, and by its else-branch if not. For the volitional multiagent transition
system above, a class of volitional machine transactions and else transactions is enabled at a
configuration in the sense of Definition 2.7 exactly when some volition-guarded transaction or
else-branch inducing it is enabled in the above sense; this determines which runs are live and
correct per Definition 2.7. Volition-change transitions belong to no class and so impose no liveness
obligation; personal choices remain free.
   A protocol F is a family of multiagent transition systems, F (𝑃) for each finite 𝑃 ⊂ Π, over
a common local-states function assigning to each 𝑃 a set of local states containing the initial
state [28, 50]. A set of volition-guarded transactions with a transaction equivalence over a local-
states function thus defines a protocol: it assigns to each 𝑃 the volitional multiagent transition
system it induces over 𝑃.

2.4    Implementations
An implementation relates a specification transition system to one that implements it, via a mapping
of configurations.
   Definition 2.14 (Implementation (Refinement) [28, 48, 58]). Given two transition systems 𝑇 𝑆 =
(𝐶, 𝑐 0,𝑇 , ∼), the specification, and 𝑇 𝑆 ′ = (𝐶 ′, 𝑐 0′ ,𝑇 ′, ∼′ ), an implementation of 𝑇 𝑆 by 𝑇 𝑆 ′ is a function
𝜎 : 𝐶 ′ → 𝐶 with 𝜎 (𝑐 0′ ) = 𝑐 0 , in which case the pair (𝑇 𝑆 ′, 𝜎) is an implementation of 𝑇 𝑆. Given
a computation 𝑟 ′ = 𝑐 1′ , 𝑐 2′ , . . . of 𝑇 𝑆 ′ , 𝜎 (𝑟 ′ ) is the computation 𝜎 (𝑐 1′ ), 𝜎 (𝑐 2′ ), . . . with consecutive
repetitions removed; a transition 𝑐 ′ → 𝑑 ′ of 𝑇 𝑆 ′ with 𝜎 (𝑐 ′ ) = 𝜎 (𝑑 ′ ) is a stutter, contributing no
step to 𝜎 (𝑟 ′ ).
   Definition 2.15 (Correct and Complete Implementation [28, 48, 58]). An implementation (𝑇 𝑆 ′, 𝜎) of
𝑇 𝑆 is correct if 𝜎 maps every correct run of 𝑇 𝑆 ′ to a correct run of 𝑇 𝑆, and complete if every correct
run 𝑟 of 𝑇 𝑆 is 𝜎 (𝑟 ′ ) for some correct run 𝑟 ′ of 𝑇 𝑆 ′ .
   A correct implementation is a refinement: a map from the implementing system’s configurations
to the specification’s under which every correct run of the one is a correct run of the other. The
Volition Elicitation                                                                                    11


implementations of this paper each hold for the runs of the implementing system that meet a
condition — that an initiator knows its participants, that variables are named apart, that a question
put to a person is answered — and we attach the condition to the system.
   Definition 2.16 (Fairness Condition). A fairness condition on a transition system 𝑇 𝑆 is a set 𝐹 of
runs of 𝑇 𝑆. A run of 𝑇 𝑆 under 𝐹 is correct if it is correct and in 𝐹 . Where a fairness condition refers
to infinitely many configurations of a run, for a finite run it refers to its last configuration.
  Definition 2.15 applies to a transition system with a fairness condition, whether it is implemented
or implements, with its correct runs being those that are correct under the condition: where no
condition is stated, every run is in it.

3     COMMUNICATING VOLITIONAL AGENTS
The framework of Section 2 abstracts over how a volition-guarded multiagent atomic transaction is
carried out: a 𝑘-ary transaction simultaneously updates the machine states of all its participants,
with no notion of how participating machines come to act in concert. An implementation on
networked smartphones has no such simultaneity: every exchange between two agents is a separate
asynchronous message-passing event, and a 𝑘-ary transaction must be implemented by a protocol
composed of such events.

3.1    Definition
To achieve that, we define communicating volitional agents (CVA), an implementation-ready restric-
tion of volition-guarded transactions; the implementation of volition-guarded transactions by CVA
is Section 3.2. CVA volition-guarded machine transactions are drawn from four syntactic forms:
binary discover transactions by which two agents come to know each other, by the will of both
persons; binary introduce transactions by which two agents that a common peer introduced come
to know each other, with no person asked; binary communicate transactions that move a message
from one agent’s outbox to another’s inbox; and unary app transactions. Thus, a CVA platform
need only specify its app transactions. Moreover every CVA protocol is volitionally grassroots
(Proposition 3.6).
Machine states. A CVA agent’s machine state has four components: a set of known peers with
whom the agent can communicate, an outbox of messages awaiting delivery, an inbox of messages
received, and an app state in which each platform specifies whatever data it requires (friend sets,
groups, coins, feeds, and so on). A message is a triple of sender, recipient, and cargo; outboxes and
inboxes are sets of messages. The cargo space 𝐶 and the app state space 𝐴 are parameters of the
CVA platform.
    Definition 3.1 (Messages). Given a set 𝐶 of cargoes, the set of messages over 𝑃 ⊂ Π is
                           𝑀 (𝑃) := {message(𝑠, 𝑟, 𝑐) | 𝑠 ≠ 𝑟 ∈ 𝑃, 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶},
with message(𝑠, 𝑟, 𝑐) a message of sender 𝑠, recipient 𝑟 , and cargo 𝑐.
 A message goes to another agent: an agent changes its own state by an app transaction, with no
message.
  Definition 3.2 (CVA Machine States and Configurations). Given messages over 𝑃 and an app state
space 𝐴 with initial state 𝑎 0 ∈ 𝐴, a CVA machine state over 𝑃 is a tuple
                        (known, 𝑜, 𝑖, 𝑎) ∈ 𝑆 (𝑃) := 2𝑃 × 2𝑀 (𝑃 ) × 2𝑀 (𝑃 ) × 𝐴,
where known ⊆ 𝑃 is a set of known peers, 𝑜 ⊆ 𝑀 (𝑃) is an outbox, 𝑖 ⊆ 𝑀 (𝑃) is an inbox, and 𝑎 ∈ 𝐴
is an app state; the initial machine state is (∅, ∅, ∅, 𝑎 0 ). A CVA machine configuration over 𝑃 is a
12                                                                                         Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


member 𝑐 ∈ 𝑆 (𝑃) 𝑃 ; for 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 we write 𝑐 𝑝 = (known𝑝 , 𝑜 𝑝 , 𝑖 𝑝 , 𝑎𝑝 ) for the machine state of 𝑝 in 𝑐.
The initial machine configuration over 𝑃 assigns the initial machine state to every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃.
  𝑃 ⊆ 𝑃 ′ =⇒ 𝑆 (𝑃) ⊆ 𝑆 (𝑃 ′ ), so that the agent states of the transition system below (Definition 2.3)
grow with 𝑃, as a protocol requires.
   Definition 3.3 (App Transaction, Effect). Given cargoes 𝐶 and an app state space 𝐴, an app trans-
action of 𝑝 is a unary volition-guarded transaction, or a volition-guarded if-then-else transaction
(Definition 2.5), over 𝑝 and CVA machine states, each of whose targets 𝑐 𝑝′ , from its source 𝑐 𝑝 , has
known𝑝′ = known𝑝 , 𝑖 𝑝′ ⊆ 𝑖 𝑝 , 𝑜 𝑝 ⊆ 𝑜 𝑝′ and 𝑜 𝑝′ \ 𝑜 𝑝 ⊆ {message(𝑝, 𝑟, 𝑥) : 𝑟 ∈ known𝑝 , 𝑥 ∈ 𝐶}, a cargo
intro(𝑞) having 𝑞 ∈ known𝑝 . Its effect eff (𝜏) is (𝑎𝑝′ , 𝑜 𝑝′ \ 𝑜 𝑝 , 𝑖 𝑝 \ 𝑖 𝑝′ ), and its else-effect eff ′ (𝜏) is the
same triple for the else-branch’s target.
   Definition 3.4 (CVA Platform). A CVA platform is a tuple (𝐶, 𝐴, 𝑎 0,𝑇 , ∼) of cargoes 𝐶, which
include the introductions intro(𝑞), 𝑞 ∈ Π, an app state space 𝐴 with initial state 𝑎 0 ∈ 𝐴, a set 𝑇 of
app transactions, and a transaction equivalence ∼ on 𝑇 , such that for 𝜏 ∼ 𝜏 ′ in 𝑇 whose sources
differ only in known, eff (𝜏) = eff (𝜏 ′ ) and eff ′ (𝜏) = eff ′ (𝜏 ′ ). The transactions of the platform over
𝑃 ⊂ Π are the members of 𝑇 over 𝑃 and, for all 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 and 𝑚 ∈ 𝑀 (𝑃), the following machine
transactions, in which nothing else changes:
(1) Discover(𝑝, 𝑞), over {𝑝, 𝑞} and guarded by {𝑝, 𝑞}: known𝑝′ = known𝑝 ∪ {𝑞} and known𝑞′ =
    known𝑞 ∪ {𝑝};
(2) Introduce(𝑝, 𝑞), over {𝑝, 𝑞}, unguarded, provided 𝑞 ∉ known𝑝 and, for some 𝑟 and 𝑟 ′ , 𝑚 =
    message(𝑟, 𝑝, intro(𝑞)) is in 𝑖 𝑝 and 𝑚 ′ = message(𝑟 ′, 𝑞, intro(𝑝)) in 𝑖𝑞 : known𝑝′ = known𝑝 ∪ {𝑞},
    known𝑞′ = known𝑞 ∪ {𝑝}, 𝑖 𝑝′ = 𝑖 𝑝 \ {𝑚} and 𝑖𝑞′ = 𝑖𝑞 \ {𝑚 ′ };
(3) Communicate(𝑝, 𝑞, 𝑚), over {𝑝, 𝑞}, unguarded, provided 𝑚 = message(𝑝, 𝑞, 𝑥) ∈ 𝑜 𝑝 and
    𝑚 ∉ 𝑖𝑞 : 𝑜 𝑝′ = 𝑜 𝑝 \ {𝑚} and 𝑖𝑞′ = 𝑖𝑞 ∪ {𝑚};
with ∼ extended to relate every Discover and Introduce of the same 𝑝 and 𝑞, and two Communicates
of the same 𝑝, 𝑞 and 𝑚.
   Definition 3.5 (CVA Agent State, Transition System, Protocol). A CVA agent state of 𝑝 is a pair
(𝑉 , 𝑠) of a volitional state 𝑉 , a set of classes of transactions guarded by 𝑝, and a CVA machine state
𝑠 ∈ 𝑆 (𝑃); a CVA agent configuration over 𝑃 assigns each 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 a CVA agent state of 𝑝. The transition
system of a CVA platform over 𝑃 is the volitional multiagent transition system its transactions over
𝑃 induce (Definition 2.12), on CVA agent configurations; the protocol of the platform assigns it to
each finite 𝑃 ⊂ Π.
   Discover, Introduce and Communicate are binary. Knowing is mutual, so Discover and Introduce
each add each of the two agents to the other’s known peers at once, and Discover is guarded
by both persons, which a unary transaction, guarded at most by its one participant, cannot be.
Communicate moves a message from one agent to the other.
   A CVA platform specifies its app transactions; Discover, Introduce and Communicate are built in.
An app transaction reads its agent’s machine state and changes only the app state, the outbox, adding
messages addressed to known peers, and the inbox, deleting messages it has read. It may require
that a peer be known, and its effect does not depend on which other peers are known. The reason
is the implementation of Section 5.4: there an agent comes to know a peer by exchanging hellos
with it, one hello at a time, so while a Discover is under way the two agents’ sets of known peers
differ from the specification’s. A test that one peer is known stays correct, and a computation over
the whole set would not. The transition system of a platform is a volitional multiagent transition
system (Definition 2.12), which requires the 𝑃-closures of two unguarded transactions of different
classes to be disjoint, and the 𝑃-closures of any two distinct CVA transactions are. Each changes
Volition Elicitation                                                                                 13


the state of every one of its participants: a binary one changes the states of both, and a unary one
the state of its one participant. Two transactions of different forms change different components:
Discover changes only the known peers; Introduce changes the known peers and the two inboxes;
Communicate changes an outbox and an inbox; and an app transaction changes the app state, the
outbox and the inbox, and not the known peers.
   An app transaction may be unguarded, no person’s will being needed for it, and its precondition
may require an arrived message. A transaction that requires a message may delete it. A guarded
app transaction that requires an arrived message may have an else-branch. Then it is taken on the
message either way, by its then-branch where its person wills it and by its else-branch where they
do not, and either branch may delete the message. A Discover carries no message: the caller has the
other’s identifier, and an implementation realises it by a cold call, carrying the caller’s identifier,
that the called person accepts, the two hellos of Section 5.4. An Introduce is taken by the machines,
with no person asked: a peer that knows two agents sends each an introduction to the other, and
the two come to know each other; it is equivalent to their Discover, and so fulfils a will to discover.
Machines come to know each other by a Discover their persons will, or by an introduction; the
platform records who is a friend, a parent or a child.
CVA protocols are volitionally grassroots. A protocol is grassroots if its groups run undisturbed
by one another and can come to interact, and volitionally grassroots if its groups run undisturbed by
one another, two groups each holding an agent that has not left can come to interact, and their first
interaction is willed by a member of each [28]; the definitions are recalled in Appendix A. Both rest
on the same fact: two agents learn of each other only by Discover, which both persons will, or by
Introduce, through a peer that already knows both, and an agent addresses only agents it knows.
   Proposition 3.6 (CVA protocols are volitionally grassroots). The protocol of every CVA
platform is grassroots and volitionally grassroots.
   The proof is in Appendix B.

3.2    Implementing the Transactions by Communicating Volitional Agents
A volition-guarded transaction changes the machine states of its 𝑘 participants at once (Section 2).
A communicating volitional agent changes only its own state, and reaches another agent only by a
message (Section 3). Here we implement the first by the second, for every local volition-guarded
specification, by two-phase commit coordinated by the agent that initiates the transaction [19].
Local specifications. In the framework of Section 2 each agent has a machine state (Definition 2.1),
and a transaction’s precondition may relate the states of its participants. In CVA a participant
reads and changes only its own state, and learns of the transaction, with its arguments, from the
initiator’s message. The implementation therefore requires two things of each part of a class, a
set of its transactions that one attempt carries out, the class being the union of its parts. First, its
precondition is a conjunction of preconditions, one on the state of each participant, which may
be the trivial one, true, and of a condition on readings, values that each participant reads off its
own state and sends with its vote. Second, its effect on each participant depends only on that
participant’s state, on the transaction’s arguments and on the readings. The implementation below
assumes this. For example, the precondition that two participants’ dates be equal is made local by
naming the date in the transaction, which the initiator sends to every participant, each checking
its own date against it; and a precondition that compares a count in one participant’s state with
a count in another’s is a condition on two readings, each participant reading its own count and
the initiator comparing them. The platforms of Section 7 are local: befriending adds each party to
the other’s friend set, provided each is absent from it, and a swap moves each party’s coins to the
other, provided each holds what it offers.
14                                                                                 Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


  The framework of Section 2 specifies a platform by a set of volition-guarded transactions and an
equivalence on them. The definition below calls these, with the machine states, a specification, and
Definition 3.8 names, for each part of a class of transactions, the set of persons whose volitions can
enable it, which is what the implementation needs.
   Definition 3.7 (Volition-Guarded Specification). A volition-guarded specification is a tuple (𝑆, 𝑠 0, 𝑅, ∼)
of machine states 𝑆 with initial state 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆, a set 𝑅 of volition-guarded transactions over 𝑆, none
of them a volition-guarded if-then-else transaction, and a transaction equivalence ∼ on their
underlying machine transactions 𝑇𝑅 , as in Definition 2.11.
   A transaction whose source no run reaches is never taken, so we take a specification with those of
its transactions (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅, 𝑡 = 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ , for which some run of the volitional multiagent transition
system it induces over some 𝑃 has a configuration 𝑐 with 𝑐𝑞𝑚 = 𝑑𝑞 for every participant 𝑞 of 𝑡; its
runs stay as they are. For a class [𝑡] ∈ 𝑇𝑅 /∼, 𝑄 ( [𝑡]) is the set of participants of its members.

one participant guards have one opener, or none; and it designates for each guarded part [𝑡] 𝑗 with
no opener a nonempty set 𝑂 ( [𝑡] 𝑗 ) ⊆ 𝐺 ( [𝑡] 𝑗 ) of its guards, its proposers, such that the guarded
parts with no opener of a class that one participant guards have the same proposers. A request of a
guard 𝑝 to an agent 𝑜 is a finite nonempty set of classes whose guarded parts that 𝑝 guards have
the opener 𝑜, or, for 𝑜 = 𝑝, a single class whose guarded parts that 𝑝 guards have no opener and 𝑝
among their proposers; a standing request of a guard 𝑝 is a decidable set of classes whose guarded
parts that 𝑝 guards have no opener and 𝑝 among their proposers.
   Under locality each participant 𝑝 has, in each part [𝑡] 𝑗 , its own precondition, the domain
         [𝑡 ]                     [𝑡 ]
dom(𝑓𝑝 𝑗 ), and its own effect, 𝑓𝑝 𝑗 , and a member of [𝑡] 𝑗 is enabled exactly when the precondition
in [𝑡] 𝑗 of every participant holds and readings of their states, one of each, meet its condition. A
                                               [𝑡 ]
part carries the transaction’s arguments, so 𝑓𝑝 𝑗 may depend on them. For example, the effect of a
swap on 𝑝 removes the coins 𝑝 offers and adds the coins 𝑝 wants, both named in the swap, and does
not read the other party’s state. A reading carries what a transaction takes from a participant’s
state beyond its arguments: where a person comes to know an AI agent and records the attestation
its principal gave it [55], which only the AI agent’s state holds, the attestation is a reading of
the AI agent’s state, and the person’s effect adds it; a state that holds several such values has
several readings, and the initiator chooses among them. A class has more than one part where its
transactions can be taken in different ways: where two friends each parent both of two children,
Volition Elicitation                                                                                 15


the befriending of the children may be approved by either parent for either child [54], and each
way is a part; and where a transaction is guarded by either of two persons, as unfriending is, its
class has two parts with the same members, one guarded by each. A guarded part has an opener
where only a participant that is not its guard can propose it: a parent of a child may close a group
the child leads, and only the child’s state lists the group’s members, so the child’s machine proposes
the closing, on the parent’s request [54]; and the guardians of a ward jointly make another person
its guardian, and only the ward’s state lists them, so the ward’s machine proposes it, on the request
of one of them, and the others are asked [16]. A request is a set of classes, since the guard need not
know which of them is to be taken: the members of the group take part in its closing, so the closing
with each list of members is a class of its own, and the parent wills the closing whatever the list.
A proposer of a part with no opener makes its request to its own machine; a guard of it that is
not a proposer is asked, as the contract’s pragmas name who proposes each act (Section 5.4). A
person may also approve standing: a standing request approves each of its classes, however many,
until the person withdraws it, a class it approves being taken once; so a person who declares the
place they are in open approves meeting everyone nearby while it is open. A standing approval is
not ended by a refusal, so its proposer proposes only where it can carry out its effect, and, after
another participant refused its attempt as unable, only once that participant tells it that it can now
carry out its own, or, after it refused its attempt on its condition, only once its own state or another
participant’s has changed.
   The protocol of a local specification assigns to each 𝑃 ⊂ Π the volitional multiagent transition
system that (𝑆, 𝑅, ∼) induces over 𝑃 (Definition 2.12), restricted to the agent configurations in which
every agent wills only classes of which it is a guard.

The protocol. A guard’s person approves a transaction once, by a request or by answering yes
when asked, and the approval stands until the transaction is taken or refused; or they approve a
set of transactions standing, by a standing request, which stands until they withdraw it, each of its
classes approved until taken. An attempt at a transaction is proposed by one of its participants, its
initiator, and names a part of the transaction’s class; each participant checks its own precondition
in that part, and sends the readings of its state with its vote yes. A part with an opener is proposed
by its opener, an unguarded one whenever the opener can carry out its effect, and a guarded one
on a request of a guard of its class, its requester, which the attempt names; the opener votes no on
such an attempt at once if it cannot carry out its effect. A guarded part with no opener is proposed
by one of its proposers whose approval stands, which votes no on it at once if it can carry out no
such part. The requester of an attempt votes yes only while its request stands. The other guards of
the part are asked: a guard whose approval stands votes without asking its person, and the persons
of the other guards must approve; where the attempt names a request, the approval that stands is
the guard’s answer yes to an attempt naming that request, so a guard is asked once of each request.
The machine of every other participant votes on its own precondition, without asking its person.
Each participant, and no other agent, votes once, yes or no, to the initiator. The initiator commits
when it holds a yes from every participant and, among the readings they sent and its own, one
of each that together meet the part’s condition, which it chooses and tells with the outcome; it
aborts on the first no, a refusal, and where no such readings meet the condition, itself then the
refuser, as unable, or when asked to yield before it holds them all. A commit ends every approval
of the transaction, and a refusal ends the approvals of the guards that voted on the attempt and
the request it names, and their persons are told who refused it and why, declined or unable; an
attempt that yields ends none, and its initiator proposes it again. A machine that cannot carry out
a transaction its person approved refuses it, so that the approval ends and the person is told. A
participant votes yes only if it can carry out its effect and holds no other attempt prepared. From its
16                                                                                 Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


yes vote until it learns the outcome, it holds the attempt prepared, and its state, with its readings,
does not change. No transaction of the protocol but Withdraw, of a standing one, withdraws an
approval. An asked participant first checks that it can carry out its effect, and only then, where no
approval of it stands, asks its person, once. It votes no at once if its person answered no; on yes, it
votes once it holds no other attempt prepared, yes if it still can and no otherwise.
   What should a participant do with a start that arrives while it holds another attempt prepared?
Voting no would lead to livelock: two attempts that reach two agents in different orders could abort
each other again and again. Waiting would lead to deadlock: two attempts could each wait for
the other. We use wound-wait [45]. A participant that holds another attempt prepared, or has a
question of the same class pending, lets the start wait, and takes waiting starts in order of priority.
The priority of an attempt is its rank, a number, the smaller rank having priority, with ties broken
by the order of identifiers, a fixed total order of the agents. Where a waiting attempt has priority
over the attempt that blocks it, the participant asks the initiator of the blocking attempt to yield,
and that initiator aborts it unless it has already decided it. An initiator proposes nothing while a
start waits in its inbox, or while a question it answered yes waits. So an attempt waits only on one
with priority over it, or on one that is yielding or has been decided and so ends without waiting,
and no cycle of waiting arises. No participant votes no because of contention: contention aborts an
attempt only by a yield to an attempt with priority over it. An attempt aborted in favour of one
with priority over it keeps its rank when its initiator proposes the part again, so only finitely many
attempts ever have priority over it, and none is starved.
    Definition 3.9 (Attempt). Fix a local volition-guarded specification. An attempt is a tuple 𝛼 =
(𝑖, 𝑘, 𝜋, [𝑡] 𝑗 , 𝑟 ) of an initiator 𝑖, a number 𝑘 ∈ N, a rank 𝜋 ≤ 𝑘, a part [𝑡] 𝑗 of a class [𝑡], and, where
[𝑡] 𝑗 is guarded and has an opener, a request 𝑟 = (𝜌, 𝑘 ′ ) of one of the guards of [𝑡], the requester
of 𝛼, numbered 𝑘 ′ , the request that 𝛼 names, and 𝑟 = ⊥ otherwise; it is an attempt of [𝑡] 𝑗 and of
[𝑡]. The initiator is the opener 𝑜 ( [𝑡] 𝑗 ) of [𝑡] 𝑗 if [𝑡] 𝑗 has one, and a proposer of [𝑡] 𝑗 if not. The
participants of 𝛼 are those of [𝑡]. Its guard set 𝐺𝛼 is 𝐺 ( [𝑡] 𝑗 ), and the members of 𝐺𝛼 \ {𝑖} other
than its requester are asked by it.
   The number identifies the attempt: each attempt an initiator proposes has a new number, larger
than every number it has seen. The rank orders the attempt: it is the number itself, or, where the
attempt retries one of the same part that was aborted in favour of an attempt with priority over it,
the rank of that one, so that the retried attempt keeps its priority.
   Definition 3.10 (Cargo, App State, Question, Approval, Free, Ready). A cargo of the protocol is
start(𝛼), vote(𝛼, 𝑦) with 𝑦 ∈ {yes, no}, a vote yes carrying the readings of its voter’s state and a vote
no its reason, declined or unable, outcome(𝛼, 𝑧) with 𝑧 ∈ {commit, abort, yield}, a commit carrying
the readings its initiator chose and an abort its refuser, the participant whose vote no refused 𝛼, with
that vote’s reason, or the initiator, which refused it on its condition, as unable, yield(𝛼), ready(𝛼),
or request(𝜌, 𝑘) with 𝜌 a request and 𝑘 ∈ N. An app state of 𝑝 is a tuple (𝑠, 𝑛, 𝐿, 𝑅) of a machine
state 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆; 𝑝’s number 𝑛 ∈ N, the largest number of an attempt it has proposed or received a start
of, or of a request it has made; a lock 𝐿, either ⊥ or the attempt 𝑝 holds prepared; and a finite record
𝑅 of terms asked(𝛼), answered(𝛼, 𝑦), voted(𝛼, 𝑦), got(𝛼, 𝑞, 𝑦), with the readings a yes carried or the
reason of a no, chose(𝛼, 𝑤), closed(𝛼), wounded(𝛼), yielded(𝛼), requested(𝜌, 𝑘), standing(𝜌, 𝑘),
ended(𝑥), readied(𝛼), ready(𝛼, 𝑞) and introduced(𝑞, 𝑟 ). An attempt 𝛼 of [𝑡] is a question of [𝑡]
pending at 𝑝 if 𝑝 has recorded asked(𝛼) and neither a vote on 𝛼 nor closed(𝛼). The approvals of 𝑝
are the requests requested(𝜌, 𝑘) it has recorded, approvals of each class of 𝜌, its standing requests
standing(𝜌, 𝑘), standing approvals of each class of 𝜌 no attempt of which has been committed at
𝑝 since, and its answers answered(𝛼, yes), approvals of the class of 𝛼; an approval is pending at
𝑝 until 𝑝 ends it, recording ended(𝜌, 𝑘) or ended(𝛼), and a standing approval is ended only by
Volition Elicitation                                                                                       17


Withdraw below. An agent 𝑝 holds a request (𝜌, 𝑘) of [𝑡] from 𝑞 if message(𝑞, 𝑝, request(𝜌, 𝑘)),
with [𝑡] ∈ 𝜌, is in its inbox. The agent 𝑝 is free for [𝑡] if 𝐿 = ⊥ and no question of [𝑡] is pending at
                                                                                                  [𝑡 ]
𝑝, and ready for a part [𝑡] 𝑗 of [𝑡] if moreover its precondition in that part holds, 𝑠 ∈ dom(𝑓𝑝 𝑗 ).
   Being free and being ready are conditions on the machine state, not on the person’s will.
   Definition 3.11 (Priority, Waiting, Blocked, Clear). Fix a total order on Π, the order of identifiers.
An attempt 𝛼 of rank 𝜋 has priority over an attempt 𝛽 of rank 𝜋 ′ if 𝜋 < 𝜋 ′ , or 𝜋 = 𝜋 ′ and the
initiator of 𝛼 precedes that of 𝛽 in the order of identifiers. A message start(𝛼), 𝛼 of [𝑡], in 𝑝’s inbox
waits at 𝑝 in three cases: while 𝑝’s inbox holds the start of an attempt with priority over 𝛼; while
a question with priority over 𝛼, answered yes, is pending at 𝑝; and while 𝑝 is not free for [𝑡]. In
the last case the start is blocked by 𝐿 if 𝐿 ≠ ⊥, and otherwise by the question of [𝑡] pending at
𝑝. A question 𝛼 pending at 𝑝 and answered yes waits while a question with priority over it and
answered yes is pending at 𝑝, and while 𝐿 ≠ ⊥, when it is blocked by 𝐿. The agent 𝑝 is clear if no
start is in its inbox and no question answered yes is pending at it.
  The app transactions of the canonical platform, defined below, use two operations on an attempt

(2) Request 𝜌 to 𝑜, by a guard 𝑝, 𝜌 a request of 𝑝 to 𝑜. Precondition: 𝑝 is free for every class of
    𝜌, ready for a guarded part of a class of 𝜌 that it guards, and 𝜌 is not a request pending at 𝑝.
    Effect: 𝑝’s number 𝑛 is increased by one, to 𝑘, 𝑝 records requested(𝜌, 𝑘), and, if 𝑜 ≠ 𝑝, adds
    message(𝑝, 𝑜, request(𝜌, 𝑘)) to 𝑜 𝑝 . Guarded by 𝑝.
(3) Stand 𝜌, by a guard 𝑝, 𝜌 a standing request of 𝑝. Precondition: 𝑝 is free for every class of 𝜌,
    and no standing approval of 𝜌 is pending at 𝑝. Effect: 𝑝’s number 𝑛 is increased by one, to 𝑘,
    and 𝑝 records standing(𝜌, 𝑘). Guarded by 𝑝.

     and 𝐿 is not an attempt of a class of 𝜌. Effect: 𝑝 records ended(𝜌, 𝑘). Guarded by 𝑝.
