   A name in a pragma is the schema’s role of that name, a name the contract’s declaration gives
a person or a ward, the schemas naming their roles as the clauses do [16], and a question names
the person asked ‘you’; a question put to a listed guarding role is put to each of its persons. The
platform fills the names from the binding of the attempt it asks about, and shows after the question
the values of the fields, by their names, and the atoms the act requires of the proposer whose
predicates the tabs list, as the tabs draw them — in a swap, the coins the proposer gives — so that
Volition Elicitation                                                                                 39


the person sees the whole proposal. A person no line names is never asked, so an act is proposed
as the person named before its button, by whoever proposes it, and the persons named before the
questions are asked: GSG’s Friendship is proposed as Alice, and Bob is asked “Alice asks to be your
friend”; GSN’s Joining and Leaving gives Remove to Alice, the leader, and Leave to Bob, a member,
and asks no one of either (Section 7). A question line naming the proposer is text of the request
form, shown with the button: the request is the will, elicited once.
   The platform the compiler prints exports, beside its agent and its dispatcher, two procedures:
contract(Text, Schemas), the contract it is compiled from, its clauses as text and its schemas
with the pragmas as the term the checker and the compiler read; and version(Current, Back),
its version and the earliest version of itself it is compatible with, both from a line of the pragmas,
so the app that loads the platform reads its contract and its versions from it [13]; a goal posted to a
program is admitted only at a procedure its root module exports [59], so the canonical compilation
(Section 6.3) writes into the root module of the program the platform is compiled for a forwarding
clause for each of the two, as it writes there the types it forms. The compiler carries the pragmas into
the platform it prints: the tab’s name and its predicate into the view the agent writes; the buttons
and the fields into the widget declaration it prints for the platform’s request type (Section 6.2),
whose term it types by type declarations it prints with the platform; and a question into the approval
question that Start raises at the role it is put to, and that a hello asks. A contract that extends or
amends others [16] has their tabs with its own, a tab of an inherited tab’s name replacing it, and an
inherited tab standing while the contract has the predicate it lists, as an inherited button stands
while the contract has an act it names, and the buttons of the acts it has: an inherited button, with
its fields and its questions, stands while the contract has an act it names, an act an amendment
deletes being gone and its button with it; and where the contract writes a button of an inherited
button’s name, its button, with its fields and its questions, replaces the inherited one, the other
buttons of the inherited line standing. The compiler takes one contract or several: of several it
prints the platform of their composition [16], for two that amend a third the schemas of the third
that both keep together with the schemas each adds, which is their union where both extend it, and
their pragmas likewise, an inherited tab or button standing as above, as the grassroots app’s own
platform is compiled from four contracts [13]; what two of them inherit from one contract is taken
once, and a schema, a tab or a button of one name that two of them write differently is refused.

6     ELICITATION: IMPLEMENTING VGLP BY GLP
Here we implement vGLP by GLP (Section 4). A question stays open until its person answers it or
the program drops its reader (Definition 5.13), and only the person’s answer reaches the goal that
asked it, so the implementation requires elicitation: offering the person, for each open question, the
means to answer it with a term of its type, and showing them what the program writes for them to
see. We derive the elicitation constructs from the interactive types, and compile vGLP onto GLP.

6.1    The Elicitation Construct
A question’s type fixes what the person is shown and what they may answer: the program writes
the positions the type gives it and the person the others, and a question reaches the person with the
program’s output in it written whole (Definition 5.5). A widget renders a type: it shows what the
program writes, takes the person’s input, and forms from it a term of the type. Widgets are associated
with types by declarations (Section 6.2), and the constructs they render are standard [40, 61].
   Definition 6.1 (Construct, Submission, Complete Widget). The construct of a question 𝑋 at agent
𝑝 is the widget associated with the interactive type of the interactive variable that is 𝑋 or whose
term holds 𝑋 , presented at 𝑝 while 𝑝’s person holds 𝑋 and 𝑋 is open. It shows the assignments
40                                                                           Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


shown at 𝑝 (Definition 5.5) to the readers of the interactive variable’s term, as they are shown, and
forms from the person’s input a term of the type of 𝑋 ; a submission of it is such a term, which
the person gives as their Answer to 𝑋 . Where 𝑋 is of type Stream(𝑇 ′ ) or List(𝑇 ′ ), a submission
gives one element and leaves the tail an open question with the same construct, or, for a list, ends
it. A widget of 𝑇 is complete if, for every question whose construct it is, every answer the Answer
transition allows for the question (Definition 5.7) is formed by some input.
  The person edits the input of a construct until they submit it; they give a submission as their
Answer, and an answer cannot be withdrawn (Definition 5.7).
   Definition 6.2 (Offered). A term 𝑡 is offered for a question 𝑋 at 𝑝 if the construct of 𝑋 presented
at 𝑝 forms 𝑡, as a submission, from some input.
   Two widgets recur, and we name questions after them, informally. A question of a tuple type is
a compose form, whose fields are the tuple’s arguments, and a question of a union of tuple types
is a menu of such forms: the volition originates with the person, who supplies the transaction’s
parameters — mint, pay, post, offer friendship. A question whose type shows content the program
writes and takes one of a few constants is an approval question, whose content is the offer and
whose buttons are the constants: the responder’s Approval of Section 1, with the proposal shown
and the buttons yes and no.
   Theorem 6.3 (Elicitation Completeness). In every configuration of a safe run of the vmaGLP
transition system over 𝑃 and 𝑀 and every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, a term offered for a question 𝑋 at 𝑝 is an answer to
𝑋 that the Answer transition allows; and if the widget of the construct of 𝑋 is complete, every answer
the Answer transition allows for 𝑋 is offered.
  The proof is in Appendix B. The first half follows from typing: a widget forms only terms of its
type, so the validation of an input is its typing. The second is a requirement on widgets, which the
default widgets below meet, the picker excepted (Proposition 6.7). A construct family draws the
widgets (Remark 6.4); nothing below the widget is proved.

6.2   Widgets
User-interface design and specification languages are a large field [1, 2, 5, 29, 37], and any of them
could be brought to bear here. For the purpose of this paper we present the basic types and their
widgets, and the declaration that associates a widget with a type.
   Remark 6.4 (Construct Families). A question’s type fixes a construct’s semantic content — what
it shows, what it takes, and the answer each submission forms — and nothing of its layout. A
construct family is a rendering of the widgets that preserves this content; the theorems of this
section, Theorem 6.3 and Theorem 6.10 below, hold for every construct family whose widgets are
complete, so families are interchangeable and each person may choose theirs — the same program
rendered plainly for one person and child-friendly for another. A family may offer in a text field
the values its person typed in it before, and may tell the device what a field takes, by the name
of its type, as an email address or a person’s name, so that the device fills it; neither changes the
answer a submission forms.
  Definition 6.5 (Widget Declaration). A widget declaration has the form 𝑇 =::= 𝑊 , where 𝑇 is a
moded type and 𝑊 a widget of the construct family that renders the program (Remark 6.4). Widget
declarations are scoped as type declarations are [59]: a declaration at the root holds for every
program, one in a module holds in that module, and a local declaration overrides a global one.
Volition Elicitation                                                                                41


  Definition 6.6 (Default Widget). The widget of a question’s type is the one named by the widget
declaration in scope where the volitional procedure asking the question is declared or, where none
does, its default widget, built from the type’s constructors:
• a constant: a button; a union of constants: a row of buttons; a position of type Constant or
  Module: a text field;
• String, a number (Integer or Real) and Key, the agent identifier: a text field, a number field
  and a peer field;
• a tuple: a form headed by its functor, with one field per argument, each labelled by the name
  of its declared type, a field of a recursive type drawn when the person opens it; a stream or list
  inside a form the person writes: submitted as a separate question and drawn under the form;
  any other union: a menu, one item per alternative, each drawn by its widget;
• a value the program writes: shown and not edited; a tuple of two arguments, a list the program
  writes and a position of its element type that the person writes: a picker in place of a form,
  offering the elements of the list;
• a stream the person writes: the widget of its element type, kept open, one element per submission;
  a list the person writes: the same, with a button that ends the list; a stream the program writes: a
  thread.
A value the program writes to its person outside a question is drawn likewise, by the widget of its
declared type.
   The mode matters: the same Stream(String) is an input box when the person writes it and
a thread when the program writes it. The widget holds the other end of the question, so its type
is the dual of 𝑇 : it writes where the program reads and reads where the program writes. The
values of a primitive type are those the runtime represents, and a field takes each of them. Every
interactive type has a default widget, so a program with no widget declarations still renders; the
default widgets are complete for their types, except the picker, which offers the elements of its list,
where the declared widgets that draw their fields and items are complete:
  Proposition 6.7 (Default Widgets). The default widget of every interactive type is complete,
but for a picker, where the declared widgets that draw its fields and items are complete.
   Proof. By induction on the answer, a finite term. The answers the Answer transition allows for
the question (Definition 5.7) are the terms of 𝑇 ′ ground at the positions the person writes, with
a fresh reader at each stream and list position and a fresh writer at each outermost position the
program writes, and, where 𝑇 ′ is Stream(𝑇 ′′ ) or List(𝑇 ′′ ), the terms [𝑒|𝑌 ?] with 𝑒 such a term
of 𝑇 ′′ , and [] for a list. A constant, or a union of constants: each answer is a constant, formed by
its button. String, Integer, Real, Key, Constant and Module: each answer is a value of the type,
formed by its field. A tuple: each answer is a tuple whose arguments are answers for their types at
the positions the person writes, formed by the form’s fields by induction, a field of a recursive type
once the person opens it, a fresh reader at a stream or list argument, which the form submits as
a separate question, and a fresh writer at a position the program writes, which the form shows
and does not edit; the form forms the tuple. Any other union: the menu selects the alternative, and
the alternative’s widget forms the answer. A stream the person writes: each answer [𝑒|𝑌 ?] has
𝑒 an answer for the element type, which holds no stream or list, formed by the element’s widget
by induction, and the widget forms the fresh tail; a list likewise, and [] by the button that ends it,
whose grant is distinct from every element. A named type, recursive or not, is its definition. A field
or an item drawn by a declared widget forms each answer of its type, that widget being complete.
A question inside the program’s output is answered by the field of its position, the default widget
of its type, by the same induction. A picker forms only the elements of its list.                    □
42                                                                              Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


   A declared widget chooses within the construct family, and Theorem 6.3 holds for it where it is
complete. It is drawn with the default widget of its type beside its name, from which it reads the
type’s constructors, so that a menu declared once lists the alternatives of the request type of each
program it is declared for; a construct family that has no widget of that name draws the default
widget. A declared widget is drawn also with the last value drawn on construct 0 (Section 6.3), and
is drawn again when that value changes, so that a request form can show the state the program
last wrote to its person. A field of a form, or an item of a menu, is drawn by the widget of its type,
declared or default. In the grassroots app (Section 8), 𝑊 names a Dart widget: the construct family
draws it, and its type fixes the terms it forms.

The words a platform shows. The words a platform compiled from a contract shows are the
contract’s pragmas (Section 5.4), and they reach the person two ways. A question put to a guarding
role names the roles of its schema, so the platform writes it into the approval question with the
persons of the attempt and the values of its binding, and its widget draws it as it draws any output
of the program. A button and a field’s name are the same for every question of their type, so the
compiler writes them into the widget declaration it prints for the request type, and a widget term
carries a label beside each constructor of Definition 6.6; a construct family draws the label it is
given, and the name of the type where it is given none.
   The compiler prints three widget declarations for a platform: for its request type, a menu of the
buttons and the fields of the contract’s acts; for its approval type, the question with the buttons yes
and no; and for Stream(Output), the type of the program’s output, a widget that draws the last
view written and the notices written after it.
   The request widget offers an act only where the person’s machine can propose it (Section 3.2), to
the person the contract names as its proposer (Section 5.4). It draws one button for each way the
viewer’s state, in the last view written, satisfies the act at the proposer’s role (Section 5.4), with the
values that way binds and the act’s fields. The other roles’ preconditions are checked at their own
machines, but for what the schemas prove of them: where every schema that adds an atom naming
𝑥 at a role requires or adds at 𝑥’s role an atom that no schema deletes, the viewer holding the first
shows that 𝑥 holds the second, and an act that forbids the second at 𝑥’s role is not offered with 𝑥. A
field that takes a person in a role offers the persons the viewer knows, since the proposer’s machine
must know every participant, other than the viewer and the persons in the act’s other roles, since
distinct roles are bound to distinct persons, but for two the clause says are possibly one person,
which one person may fill (Section 5.4), and, where that person’s role requires an atom that the
schemas prove of a person from the viewer’s records, as above, only a person so proved; the button
is drawn only where each such field has someone to offer. A field that takes a person in no role, as
the issuer wanted of a redemption, takes the person’s key and offers the persons the viewer knows,
the act not requiring the proposer to know them: it is the peer field of Definition 6.6. The field of
an acquaintance takes the key of a person the viewer does not know. A button that names several
acts is drawn once for the ways of its acts that bind the same values. How the buttons of one act
are arranged, each beside the atoms that bind it or as one button with a picker, is the construct
family’s (Remark 6.4).
   An approval question is drawn as its question, with the values and the proposer’s atoms after
it (Section 5.4), and the buttons yes and no. A refresh is a gesture or an icon, as the construct
family chooses, with no word. A speech act, in a tab, a question or a field, is drawn with its signers,
innermost first, by the names the viewer’s state holds for them. A notice is drawn as the refuser,
the reason and the act’s button — “Bob declined Befriend”, “Bob unable Befriend” — with, where
the reason is unable, the atoms the schema requires at the refuser that its state lacks and those it
forbids that its state has.
Volition Elicitation                                                                                 43


6.3    The Implementation: Compiling vGLP onto GLP
vGLP needs no new machine: it compiles onto GLP, clause by clause, into a GLP program over an
uninterpreted person channel, and Theorem 6.10 relates the runs of the two. GLP already connects
the program to the person: each agent’s first channel is to the person operating it; messages flowing
toward the person are rendered by the runtime, and the person’s responses flow back as GLP
messages [53, 58]. In the compiled program each goal of a volitional procedure, at its first reduction
attempt, has the dispatcher spawn a construct process for its question and receives its end of its
interactive variable. The bridge, the Dart side of the runtime, draws each construct’s widget and
returns the person’s acts on it, each act a grant. We first make the person’s side formal.
   Definition 6.8 (Person Channel, Person Writer, GLP with Persons, Grant). Let 𝑁 be a GLP program
and 𝑃 ⊂ Π. A person channel of a goal 𝐺 0 satisfying SO is a variable pair (𝑈 , 𝑈 ?) such that 𝑈 ?
occurs in 𝐺 0 and 𝑈 does not; 𝑈 is its person writer. GLP with persons over 𝑃 and 𝑁 , with a designated
person channel of each agent’s initial resolvent, is the transition system obtained from the maGLP
transition system over 𝑃 and 𝑁 (Definition 4.11) by adding, at each agent 𝑝, grant transitions
                              (𝐺 𝑝 , 𝜎𝑝 ) → (𝐺 𝑝 , 𝜎𝑝 ◦ {𝑈 ? := [𝑐 | 𝑈 1 ?]})
where 𝑐 is a ground term input(Id, Pos, 𝑅) or refresh, 𝑈 the person writer of 𝑝, and 𝑈 1 a
writer of V𝑝 occurring nowhere in the configuration, the person writer after the transition, every
other agent stationary; the partial equivalence is the maGLP transaction equivalence, with grant
transitions outside its domain.
   A grant is the person’s act: a tap, a keystroke or a submission on a construct grants the term
it carries, the construct Id, the position Pos and the person’s raw input 𝑅; and a gesture grants
refresh, as does the runtime when it restarts the agent from its saved state, the screen not being
part of that state. The person channel is typed by these declarations, at the root of every program:
PersonChannel(T) ::= Channel(PersonIn(T), PersonOut(T)).
PersonIn(T) ::= [Input(T) | PersonIn(T)].
PersonOut(T) ::= [Draw(T) | PersonOut(T)].
Input(T) ::= input(Integer, Path, T) ; refresh.
Path ::= [] ; [Integer | Path].
Draw(T) ::= draw(Integer, Widget, T) ; withdraw(Integer).
Neither stream ends: the person and the program stay connected, and a goal standing for the
person, as in a test, waits on the draws when it has nothing to grant. The parameter T is the type of
the values drawn and of the person’s raw inputs, which the compilation forms (Appendix B, The
construct process); Widget is the type of the terms that draw widgets: the terms of the default
widgets, built from the constructors of Definition 6.6, each carrying the label its widget declaration
gives it, and, for a declared widget, the term named(𝑊 , 𝐷) of the widget 𝑊 its declaration gives, a
term of the construct family, and the default widget 𝐷 of its type. Definition 5.10 (proper run) applies
to GLP with persons verbatim, with no held variables and grants leaving the goals unchanged, and
properness is the fairness condition of GLP with persons: nothing is required of the person, since
the machine owes nothing to a question its person leaves open.
   The compilation is defined for every vGLP program 𝑀. A goal of a volitional procedure 𝑞 with
interactive type 𝑇 , called with its 𝑛 arguments, first reduces with an asking clause, which sends
an ask to the dispatcher and calls the guarded clauses of 𝑞, under a fresh name, with its end of a
new interactive variable of type 𝑇 added; this reduction is the Ask. Asks travel on ask streams:
every procedure that may reach a question carries one, and a clause merges the ask streams of
its body goals into its own. On an ask the dispatcher spawns the construct process of the question,
44                                                                              Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


which holds the person’s end of the variable: the dispatcher routes the person’s grants to it by
its identifier; once the output the program writes into the variable is written whole, it has the
bridge draw the widget of 𝑇 with that output; and it binds each question it holds with the term
the person’s input forms, one element per submission for a stream type. The construct process
withdraws when every question it holds is answered or closed. The dispatcher takes asks, grants
and draws in turn, so a grant is routed and a draw is passed on however many asks arrive; on
refresh it has every construct process, and construct 0, draw again what it last drew, so the screen
is redrawn whole. A question whose reader the program drops is closed, and the construct process
takes it off the screen: GLP’s guard widowed (𝑊 ) succeeds on a writer 𝑊 whose reader has been
dropped and occurs nowhere in the agent’s state [53].
The canonical compilation. Let 𝑀 be a vGLP program. A procedure of 𝑀 reaches a question
if it is volitional or a clause of it calls a procedure that does; each such procedure has one more
argument, last, its ask stream, which its goal writes and the dispatcher reads. In a clause of such a
procedure, each call in the body of a procedure that reaches a question is given a fresh writer as
its ask stream, and the head carries 𝐷?, where 𝐷 is the clause’s ask stream, with the body’s ask
streams merged into 𝐷: the call is given 𝐷 itself where there is one such call, merge goals merge
them where there are more, and the head carries [] in place of 𝐷? where there is none.
    For a volitional procedure 𝑞 of 𝑀 with interactive type 𝑇 , the asking clause of 𝑞, under the name
𝑞 so that every call of 𝑞 reaches it unchanged, is
q(S1, ..., Sn, [ask(T, t(X))|D?]) :- q1(S1', ..., Sn', X?, D).
where 𝑆𝑙′ passes the argument on: at an input position the head carries 𝑆𝑙 and 𝑆𝑙′ is its reader 𝑆𝑙 ?, and
at an output position the head carries 𝑆𝑙 ? and 𝑆𝑙′ is the writer 𝑆𝑙 . X and X? are exchanged where 𝑇 is in
writer mode. t is the name of 𝑇 in lowercase, the names of its type arguments, so formed, appended,
with _w for writer mode and _r for reader mode, as in approval_w and stream_request_r. Where
two modules of 𝑀 define types of one name, the name of each is prefixed with the path of its
module, the names of its directories and its file joined by _, as in parent_approval_w. So the asks
of 𝑀 are of one type, the union over those functors.
   The canonical compilation ⌈𝑀⌉ of a vGLP program 𝑀 is the GLP program consisting of:
• every ordinary clause of 𝑀, extended with its ask streams (above);
• for each volitional procedure 𝑞 of 𝑀, its clauses as guarded clauses of arity 𝑛+1 under a fresh
  name, q1 above, extended, and its asking clause;
• for each interactive type 𝑇 of 𝑀, the construct process of 𝑇 , which the dispatcher spawns on an
  ask with its variable and its channels to the dispatcher (Appendix B);
• the dispatcher, which reads the ask stream of the initial goal and the person channel, spawns
  a construct process with a fresh identifier on each ask, and routes the person’s grants to the
  construct processes by identifier, by the standard stream techniques (Appendix B prints it).
The initial goal of ⌈𝑀⌉ at each agent is that of 𝑀, extended, and the dispatcher on the ask stream
of that goal and on the person channel, which is its person side.
   A vGLP program is organised in modules as a GLP program is [59]. A .vglp source in the
directory tree of a program is compiled into a module of that program, as a .glp source is, and
the self.vglp of a directory holds the procedures, volitional or not, and the widget declarations
shared by every module in that directory and below it, as its self.glp holds those of GLP; the
self.vglp of the root holds widget declarations only. A procedure that reaches a question is
exported, imported and shared as any procedure is, with its ask stream added, so the questions of
a program are those of all its modules. The compilation forms the type of the asks, the construct
processes and the dispatcher for the linked program, and the GLP module that forms the program’s
Volition Elicitation                                                                               45


initial goal imports the compiled procedures it calls, ask streams included, and the dispatcher.
The compilation is typed: ⌈𝑀⌉ carries the type definitions and procedure declarations of 𝑀, each
procedure that reaches a question declared with its ask stream added, a volitional procedure’s
declaration being its asking clause’s and its guarded clauses declared under their fresh name with
the argument of its interactive type added in the mode of that type, the type of the asks, and the
declarations of the construct processes and the dispatcher. Every module of ⌈𝑀⌉ type-checks as
GLP [59] with no type written by hand: the compilation writes into each module that imports a
compiled procedure, the dispatcher, or a procedure whose declaration names a type the compilation
forms, the types it forms for them, the linker so writing them, among them the type of the asks,
the view types and the parameter of the person channel (Appendix B). A GLP module that calls a
compiled procedure is a module of the vGLP program, every GLP program being a vGLP program,
and is compiled with it.
   A vGLP program declares the type of its output to its person by defining the type Output,
a reserved name; the compiled program draws that output, construct 0, by the widget of its
type Stream(Output), by default a thread whose elements are drawn by the widget of Output
(Definition 6.6), and the dispatcher merges these draws with the constructs’. A program that writes
nothing to its person defines no Output and draws no construct 0. A program that writes to its
person and defines no Output is refused, and so is one whose Output has a position the person
writes. Construct 0 is drawn when the program starts, and the dispatcher numbers the questions
from 1.
   The asking clause passes each argument of the goal on through a pair of its own, so until the
pair’s assignment is communicated a compiled clause that reads the argument suspends; a later
clause that does not read it could then be taken where the source goal takes the earlier one, and
the restriction on the clauses of a volitional procedure (Definition 5.2) excludes it. At an output
position the asking clause’s head carries a reader, which a writer of the goal matches and a term
of the goal that is not a writer does not, so the calls of a volitional procedure hold writers there
(Definition 5.2), and a goal keeps them until it is asked, holding their one occurrence.
   The construct process is the program’s end of the widget: it forms from the person’s input a
term of its question’s type, so the type checker [59] guarantees that a question is bound only with
a well-typed term. The bridge draws the widget and reports raw input; everything crossing to Dart
is a ground term or a construct identifier, and answers and channels stay on the GLP side.

   Definition 6.9 (Implementation of vGLP by GLP, Correct, Complete). The implementation of vGLP
by GLP is the function taking a vGLP program 𝑀 to its canonical compilation ⌈𝑀⌉ (Section 6.3). It
is correct if for every 𝑀 and 𝑃 ⊂ Π some 𝜎 is a correct implementation of the vmaGLP transition
system over 𝑃 and 𝑀, under the fairness condition that its runs are proper, by GLP with persons
over 𝑃 and ⌈𝑀⌉, and complete for 𝑀 if for every 𝑃 some such 𝜎 is also complete.

  Theorem 6.10 (Compilation). The implementation of vGLP by GLP is correct, and complete for
every program whose interactive types have complete widgets.

   The proof, with the mapping 𝜎, is in Appendix B. Correctness is per transition and then per class:
the asking clause’s reduction is the Ask, a construct process binding its question with an answer is
the Answer, its draw of a question’s output is the Communicate of that output to the person, and
every other step of a construct process, of the dispatcher, of a merge goal the compilation adds and
of a grant is a stutter; a class enabled forever is taken, the asking clauses and the compiled clauses
being GLP clauses. Completeness is by construction: for each Answer of the given run, the person
grants the input that forms its term, which a complete widget allows. The compilation is per agent,
46                                                                             Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


the person channel being local to its agent, and Communicate and Cold-call are carried unchanged
by the multiagent GLP runtime [53, 58].
The implementation. The standard GLP implementation, extended with the compiler of the
canonical compilation and the Dart bridge rendering the constructs, implements vGLP: a vGLP
program is compiled into its canonical compilation, the engine runs it unchanged, and the bridge
draws each construct’s widget and returns the person’s raw input as grants. The composite —
engine, compiler, construct processes, dispatcher and bridge — is the implementation of vGLP;
Section 7 shows each platform’s requests and approval questions.
   We claim, informally, that the composite is GLP with persons over the compiled program: a tap,
a keystroke or a submission on a construct is a grant, and the engine is the GLP runtime of [58].
One step is serial: the person’s raw input reaches the GLP side on one stream, and the dispatcher
routes it to the construct processes by identifier.

6.4   The Cascade
The implementations of Sections 3.2, 5.4 and 6.3 compose. For an effective local specification
(Definitions 3.8 and 5.16), let Θ be its canonical platform with a computable introduction rule or
none, ⌈Θ⌉ the compilation of Θ into vGLP, and ⌈⌈Θ⌉⌉ the canonical compilation of that program onto
GLP; let 𝜎3 be the compilation mapping (Definition B.2), 𝜎2 the platform mapping (Definition 5.17),
and 𝜎1 the CVA implementation (Definition B.1).
   Theorem 6.11 (Cascade). For every effective local volition-guarded specification, computable
introduction rule or none, and 𝑃 ⊂ Π, 𝜎1 ◦ 𝜎2 ◦ 𝜎3 is a correct implementation of F (𝑃) by GLP with
persons over 𝑃 and ⌈⌈Θ⌉⌉, under the fairness condition that a run 𝜌 is proper, that no approval question
stays open in 𝜎3 (𝜌), and that 𝜎2 (𝜎3 (𝜌)) is connected, unobstructed, steady and resolute.
   The proof is in Appendix B. The fairness condition asks of the persons that they answer the
approval questions put to them, and of the run that one who can propose a transaction willed and
not taken knows its other parties, that no agent is kept busy without end, and that a class willed
and not taken has, from some point on, one part that holds with all its guards for the class. It does
not ask for the strong fairness of Definition 3.22: the compiled agent takes a person’s request at the
dispatch that reads it, and a proposal among the own transactions it takes, one on each own trigger,
until none holds, so a Request or a Propose enabled again and again is taken again and again.
   The protocol of GLP with persons over ⌈⌈Θ⌉⌉ is defined as that of ⌈Θ⌉ in Section 5.4, with GLP
with persons in place of vmaGLP.
  Proposition 6.12 (Grassroots Cascade). For every effective local volition-guarded specification
with acquaintance parts guarded by every two agents, and computable introduction rule or none, the
protocol of GLP with persons over ⌈⌈Θ⌉⌉ is grassroots.
  The proof is in Appendix B.
  The results of the paper, and the conditions each places on the runs of the implementing system,
are these:
          Result             Arrow       Condition on the runs
          Proposition 3.20               safe; for termination, also live
          Theorem 3.24       (3) → (4)   connected, unobstructed, steady, resolute and strongly
                                         fair
          Theorem 5.19       (4) → (5)   proper, no approval question staying open, and its im-
                                         age connected, unobstructed, steady and resolute
          Theorem 6.10       (5) → (6)   proper
          Theorem 6.11       (3) → (6)   proper, no approval question staying open, and its im-
                                         age in P connected, unobstructed, steady and resolute
Volition Elicitation                                                                                    47


7        GRASSROOTS PLATFORMS
This section presents three grassroots platforms — the grassroots social graph, grassroots social
network, and grassroots currencies — as the contracts the compiler of Section 5.4 compiles: GSG and
GSN [16], and GC [49]. Each contract is in a box of its own, its text the repository’s copy, opening
with its relation to the contract it is written from [16] — it extends a contract when it adds schemas
to it, and amends it when it also deletes some — and with the declaration of its names, and cited by
the contract’s name and a clause’s number, as GSG:2; it cites the clauses of the contract it is written
from and does not restate them. The schemas of each contract are in a second box, numbered by its
clauses: a schema is an implication, its head what the act adds and, negated, deletes, its body what
the act requires and, negated, forbids, the first argument of each atom the person who holds it,
guarded by the persons who must will the act [16]. Beside the clauses of each contract the repository
holds its speech-act schemas [16], one for each act a clause describes, by which the contract is a
local specification (Definition 3.8), and its pragmas, the words its app shows, printed in its box below
its clauses, from which the compiler takes the words of the platform it prints (Section 5.4). A clause
opens with its legal modality [16]: enforced, attested, undertaken. The CVA implementation
of each is its canonical CVA platform (Definition 3.17), correct by Theorem 3.24, and, the contract
being effective (Definition 5.16), the compiler checks it and prints the compilation of that platform
into vGLP (Section 5.4), correct by Theorem 5.19. A binary transaction guarded by both parties is
then the two-phase commit over two participants (Section 3.2): an offer is a request of one party to
their own machine, which proposes it; an acceptance is the other party’s Answer to the approval
question that Start raises, and the proposing machine commits on receipt of the acceptance’s vote
and tells the other party; the person’s no to it takes Answer’s else-branch.
   The programs are in the public GLP repository, https://github.com/EShapiro2/GLP, under programs/:
GSG and GSN, with their schemas, in gsc, and GC in currencies/coins. The apps of GKK, GSG,
GSN and GC are in apps (Section 8). A run of the cascade on each contract is recorded in cascade/:
its clauses and schemas, the checker’s verdict, the compiled program in vGLP and in GLP, and
the log of its run with scripted persons, which the test suite repeats. Programs written earlier, by
hand, are in social/graph (the social graph, one-to-one messaging and the super-app [13]), in
currencies (the coins agent, in the syntax of Section 5) and in cssn (the child-safe network, in an
earlier syntax of vGLP). A .vglp source in the syntax of Section 5 is compiled by the canonical
compilation of Section 6.3 into the .glp file beside it. The requests below are given in the syntax of
Section 5.

7.1       Grassroots Social Graph
In a grassroots social network [51], the social graph is stored distributively under the control of
the people themselves, with each person storing the local neighbourhood pertaining to them, and
no third-party having access unless explicitly granted. A person is a natural person of age [16]. A
person takes the pronoun ‘they’; for readability we write ‘she’ for Alice and ‘he’ for Bob. Every
contract extends GKK, Grassroots Keys and Knowledge [16], and cites its clauses whole; a person
knows another by holding their public key.

         GKK: Grassroots Keys and Knowledge [16]
    Alice, Bob and Carol are persons.
    1.     Identity. Alice may establish her identity once by choosing a unique key pair. She makes
         herself known by the public key, signs her acts with the private key, and is accountable for
         every act signed with it.
48                                                                           Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro



     2.    Acquaintance. Alice and Bob may come to know each other by an act of both.
     3.    Introduction. If Alice and Bob know each other and Alice knows Carol, then Alice can
          make Carol known to Bob, with Bob’s consent.
      Pragmas
     Contacts: the persons Alice knows.
     Identity: Alice [Establish]
     Acquaintance: Alice [Acquaint] <person>
         Bob: Alice wishes that you know each other.
     Introduction: Alice [Introduce]
         Bob: Alice asks to introduce Carol to you.

     GSG extends GKK with friendship; its box defines trust.

          GSG: Grassroots Social Graph [16]
     GSG extends GKK [16].
     Alice and Bob are persons. Alice trusts Bob when she knows Bob personally and believes him
     to keep the undertakings he signs under grassroots contracts.
     1.  Friendship. Alice and Bob, who trust each other and are not friends, may jointly decide to
       become friends.
     2. Ending a friendship. Alice may end her friendship with Bob.
     3. Loss of Trust. Alice ends her friendship with Bob when she ceases to trust him.
      Pragmas
     Friends: the friends of Alice.
     Friendship: Alice [Befriend]
         Bob: Alice asks to be your friend.
     Ending a friendship: Alice [Unfriend]

   GSG is the grassroots social graph, introduced and analysed in [28, 51].
The schemas. The schemas of GKK are over two predicates: identity, of arity zero in a state, an
identity established under GKK:1, and knows, of arity one, knows(Bob) in the state of Alice saying
that Alice holds Bob’s public key. GSG adds friend, of arity one, friend (Bob) in the state of Alice
saying that Bob is a friend of Alice. Every role of every schema but that of GKK:1 requires identity,
which the boxes show only there. The schemas are named for their acts: establish, acquaint and
introduce of GKK, in which Carol is bound to an agent, and befriend and unfriend of GSG.

          GKK: Speech-Act Schemas [16]

     1. identity(Alice) ←
             ¬identity(Alice) guarded by Alice.
     2. knows(Alice, Bob), knows(Bob, Alice) ←
             guarded by Alice, Bob.
     3. knows(Bob, Carol) ←
             knows(Alice, Bob), knows(Alice, Carol), knows(Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice, Bob.
Volition Elicitation                                                                               49


       GSG: Speech-Act Schemas [16]

  1. friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) ←
          knows(Alice, Bob), ¬friend (Alice, Bob), knows(Bob, Alice), ¬friend (Bob, Alice)
          guarded by Alice, Bob.
  2. ¬friend (Alice, Bob), ¬friend (Bob, Alice) ←
          friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice.

Befriending is guarded by both, so the act takes the will of both. Unfriending is guarded by Alice,
and its class has two parts, at the binding of either friend to Alice, each guarded by that friend
(Section 5.4). The accountability GKK:1 states and the trust GSG:1 requires describe no act, and
GSG:3, undertaken, describes none: it binds Alice to GSG:2 when she ceases to trust Bob.
vGLP implementation. The compilation of the canonical platform (Section 5.4). The agent’s
request type has the alternative propose(𝑁 ) for each request name 𝑁 , a schema-binding of
establish, of acquaint, introduce, befriend or unfriend with a peer, and an acquaintance with a peer
whose key the person has is also the cold call by which their machines come to know each other
(Section 5.4). The approval question Start raises on an arriving offer of friendship shows the
offering peer and takes yes or no, no being the refusal; the approval questions of acquaintance and
of introduction show the peer, and the peer introduced, likewise. The request form is a menu of the
requests.

7.2     Grassroots Social Network
GSN amends GSG [16, 51]: it deletes GSG’s unfriend and adds its own, which also ends every
membership between the two, with messages and groups. In the repository its clauses speak of
agents: an agent is a person, a child or a computational (AI) agent [16].

       GSN: Grassroots Social Network [16]
  GSN amends GSG [16].
  Alice and Bob are persons.
  1.  Messaging. Alice may send a message to her friend Bob.
  2.  Group. Alice may create a group, of which she is the leader and first member; Alice may
    close the group by leaving it.
  3. Joining and Leaving. Alice, the leader of a group, may add her friend Bob to it, with Bob’s
    consent; either Alice or Bob may then end Bob’s membership in the group, which also ends
    when Alice and Bob cease to be friends.
  4. Group messaging. A member of a group may send a message to the group, and its leader
    delivers it to every current member.
      Pragmas
  Groups: the groups Alice is a member of.
  Messages: the messages Alice received.
  Posts: the posts Alice received in her groups.
  Messaging: Alice [Message] <text>
  Group: Alice [Create] <name> [Close]
  Joining and Leaving: Alice [Join]
      Bob: Alice asks you to join her group.
50                                                                                   Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro



        Alice [Remove]
        Bob [Leave]
     Group messaging: Bob [Post] <text>
        Alice [Post own] <text>

The schemas. A group is named by the community name ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛 of its leader Alice and a speech
act 𝑛 Alice signs [16]: member (⟨Alice⟩ ·𝑛) in the state of Bob says that Bob is a member of the group,
and in the state of Alice listed (Bob, 𝑛) says the same, closed (𝑛) that the group has ended, posted (𝑥, 𝑛)
that 𝑥 was posted to it and delivered (𝑥, Bob, 𝑛) that Alice delivered 𝑥 to Bob in it; received (𝑥, Alice)
in the state of Bob is a speech act Bob received from Alice, and received_in(𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) one Alice
delivered to Bob in the group. The schemas, in the box’s order, are message of GSN:1; create and
close of GSN:2; join, leave, remove and GSN’s unfriend of GSN:3; and post_own, post and deliver of
GSN:4.

       GSN: Speech-Act Schemas [16]

     1. received (Bob, 𝑥, Alice) ←
              friend (Alice, Bob) guarded by Alice.
     2. member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛) ←
              ¬member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), ¬closed (Alice, 𝑛) guarded by Alice.
        closed (Alice, 𝑛), ¬listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛), ¬member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛),
        ∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛),
              ∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)) guarded by Alice.
     3. listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), friend (Alice, Bob), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)
              guarded by Alice, Bob.
        ¬listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) ←
              listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) guarded by Bob.
        ¬listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) ←
              listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) guarded by Alice.
        ¬friend (Alice, Bob), ¬listed (Alice, Bob, _), ¬member (Alice, ⟨Bob⟩ · _), ¬friend (Bob, Alice),
        ¬listed (Bob, Alice, _), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · _) ←
              friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice.
     4. posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), ¬posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛) guarded by Alice.
        posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), friend (Alice, Bob), ¬posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛),
              member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) guarded by Bob.
        delivered (Alice, 𝑥, Bob, 𝑛), received_in(Bob, 𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛), listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), friend (Alice, Bob),
              ¬delivered (Alice, 𝑥, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) unguarded.

Close quantifies over every Bob that Alice lists, its listed role, so the leader ends the group and
every membership in it in one act. The wildcard in GSN’s unfriend clears, at each of the two, the
other’s listing in a group they lead and their own membership in a group the other leads. Delivery
Volition Elicitation                                                                                  51


is unguarded: the leader undertook it on creating the group. A post and a delivery require that the
member be the leader’s friend, so a group’s messages travel between friends.

vGLP implementation. The compilation of the canonical platform (Section 5.4). The schema-
bindings of message, create, close, join, leave, remove, unfriend and the two posts are request names,
alternatives propose(𝑁 ) of the agent’s request type. A join the leader proposes raises an approval
question at the friend, which shows the group and its leader and takes yes or no. The leader’s agent
proposes a close, reading the members from its own state. Deliver, unguarded, is proposed by the
leader’s machine, its opener, and voted on by the member’s. Posting is a request, one message per
submission, as the chat of Section 5 is.

7.3     Grassroots Currencies
Grassroots coins [27, 49] are units of debt that can be issued and traded digitally by any person.
Each person’s coins are backed by the goods and services they offer, priced in their own currency,
with liquidity arising from mutual credit via coin exchange among persons that know and trust
each other. A party is a person, natural or legal (with the gender-neutral pronoun ‘they’), with
Alice, Bob and Carol as variables naming distinct parties to the contract. A grassroots coin is a unit
of debt that names its issuer, with Alice-coin referring to a grassroots coin issued by Alice.


       GC: Grassroots Coins [49]
  GC extends GKK [16].
  Alice and Bob are persons.
  1.  Minting. Alice may decide to issue Alice-coins.
  2.  Swap. Alice and Bob, who know each other, may jointly decide to swap coins they hold.
  3.  Payment. Alice may decide to pay Bob-coins they hold to Bob, whom they know.
  4.  Redemption. Alice may decide to redeem a Bob-coin they hold against any coin held by
    Bob, whom they know.
  5. Pricing. Alice prices their offerings in Alice-coins and accepts them in payment at those
    prices.
      Pragmas
  Coins: the coins Alice holds.
  Minting: Alice [Mint] <amount>
  Swap: Alice [Swap] <amount given> <issuer wanted> <amount wanted>
     Bob: Alice asks to swap coins with you.
  Payment: Alice [Pay] <amount>
  Redemption: Alice [Redeem] <issuer wanted>


The schemas. GC extends GKK (Section 7.1). Its schemas [16, 49] are over one predicate beside
GKK’s, ¢, of arity one in a state, ¢(𝑢) in the state of Alice being a coin issued by 𝑢 and held by Alice,
as many copies as Alice holds such coins; in the repository the predicate is coin. An amount is a
multiplicity, 𝑘 · 𝜑 being 𝑘 copies of the atom 𝜑, and an atom negated in a head with a multiplicity 𝑘
deletes 𝑘 copies (Section 5.4). The schemas are mint, swap, pay and redeem, of GC:1 to GC:4.
52                                                                                  Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


       GC: Speech-Act Schemas [16]

     1. 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, Alice) ←
               guarded by Alice.
     2. 𝑘 1 · ¢(Alice, 𝑣), knows(Alice, 𝑣), ¬𝑘 · ¢(Alice, 𝑢), 𝑘 · ¢(Bob, 𝑢), knows(Bob, 𝑢), ¬𝑘 1 · ¢(Bob, 𝑣)
        ←
               knows(Alice, Bob), 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, 𝑢), knows(Bob, Alice), 𝑘 1 · ¢(Bob, 𝑣) guarded by Alice,
               Bob.
     3. ¬𝑘 · ¢(Alice, Bob), 𝑘 · ¢(Bob, Bob) ←
               knows(Alice, Bob), 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, Bob) guarded by Alice.
     4. ¬¢(Alice, Bob), ¢(Alice, 𝑟 ), knows(Alice, 𝑟 ), ¬¢(Bob, 𝑟 ), ¢(Bob, Bob) ←
               knows(Alice, Bob), ¢(Alice, Bob), ¢(Bob, 𝑟 ) guarded by Alice.

Minting adds 𝑘 Alice-coins at Alice. A swap, guarded by both, who know each other, moves 𝑘 coins
of 𝑢 from Alice to Bob and 𝑘 1 coins of 𝑣 from Bob to Alice. A payment moves 𝑘 Bob-coins from
Alice to Bob, and a redemption moves a Bob-coin from Alice to Bob and a coin of 𝑟 from Bob to
Alice. Whoever receives a coin comes to know its issuer. GC:5 describes no act.
vGLP implementation. The compilation of the canonical platform (Section 5.4): every schema
is guarded, so each schema-binding is a request name, an alternative propose(𝑁 ) of the agent’s
request type whose arguments the request form takes. A swap raises an approval question at
the other party, which shows the coins given and the coins taken and takes yes or no. The other
party’s machine votes no on a swap whose wanted coins it does not hold, without asking its person.
It votes on a payment and on a redemption by its own precondition, and its person is asked by
neither. The coins agent of the repository, in currencies/coins, written earlier by hand, has
the requests mint(Amount), swap(Friend, GiveCoin, GiveAmount, WantCoin, WantAmount),
pay(Friend, Amount) and redeem(Friend, WantCoin), and the holdings are a list the program
writes to the person.
   The agent’s request form is a menu of its requests, each a form whose fields are the request’s
arguments, so the person’s input is the term the agent consumes.
Effective specifications. The three contracts meet the conditions of Section 5.4, which the compiler
checks: every variable of a schema occurs in an atom it deletes or adds; GSN’s unguarded schema,
deliver, names the member, the message and the group in atoms of the leader’s state; and GSN’s
close has one listed role, whose base, the leader, is its one guarding role. So each is an effective
local specification, and its canonical platform compiles into vGLP (Section 5.4).

8     THE GRASSROOTS APP
The apps of GKK, GSG, GSN and GC are compiled from their contracts down the cascade (Section 7)
and run as mini-apps of the grassroots super-app [13] on an iPhone simulator; Figure 2 shows
them. Each app’s directory in the repository, programs/apps/gkk, gsg, gsn and gc, holds the
canonical CVA platform the compiler of Section 5.4 prints from the contract’s schemas and pragmas,
platform.vglp, which the canonical compilation (Section 6.3) compiles to GLP when the directory
loads, with the three widget declarations the compiler prints (Section 6.2); the mini-app, which
wraps the compiled agent and its dispatcher; and a scripted person. Every element of a screen is a
construct (Definition 6.1) or the program’s output: the request question, drawn as the tabs’ buttons
and fields, a button beside each row it names; the approval questions, each drawn at the top of its
tab as its question with yes and no; and the view the agent writes, drawn as the tabs’ lists, a row for
Volition Elicitation                                                                                 53


each atom of the tab’s predicate, with the notices. Every word on a screen is the contract’s or one of
the platform’s four, yes, no, declined and unable (Section 6.2); the app’s title is the contract’s name.
GKK. (1) Alice’s Contacts tab, the tab of GKK, before she establishes her identity: Establish, and
Acquaint with its field person, for the key of a peer not listed. (2) Alice typed Bob’s key and tapped
Acquaint: her request is the cold call, which asks Bob “Alice wishes that you know each other.” with
yes and no at the top of his Contacts tab (Section 5.4). (3) Bob’s Contacts tab after he answered yes
to “Alice asks to introduce Carol to you.”: a row for each person he knows, Carol and Alice, each
with Meet and Introduce.
GSG. (4) Alice, who met Bob, tapped Befriend beside him: Bob’s Friends tab, the tab of GSG, with
“Alice asks to be your friend.” and yes and no. (5) Alice’s Friends tab after the befriend commits: Bob,
with Meet, Introduce, Befriend and Unfriend. (6) Alice’s Contacts tab after Bob ends the friendship:
Bob listed there, and her Friends tab lists no one.
GSN. (7) Bob’s chat with Alice in his Friends tab, her message “Hi Bob” with her name under it,
delivered under GSN:1, and the field text with Message. (8) Bob’s Groups tab, the tab of GSN, with
“Alice asks you to join her group.”, the group’s name, friends, and yes and no. (9) Bob’s Groups tab
with the group friends, led by Alice, and his message “Hi all” as Alice delivered it to him under
GSN:4, with Post and Leave. (10) Alice’s Groups tab with the group she leads, its members Bob and
Alice, Bob’s “Hi all” and her “Welcome”, with Join, Remove, Close and Post own.
GC. (11) Alice typed 2 into Mint’s field amount and tapped Mint: her Coins tab, the tab of GC, with
a row for her coins, Alice, and their count, 2. (12) Alice asked Bob to swap her two Alice-coins for
his three Bob-coins: his Coins tab with “Alice asks to swap coins with you.”, the binding’s values
under it — issuer given Alice, amount given 2, issuer wanted Bob, amount wanted 3 — and yes
and no, and his row, Bob 3. (13) Bob answered yes and the swap committed: his Coins tab with the
Alice-coins he holds, Alice 2, with Pay and Redeem; Swap is in the rows of his Contacts tab. (14)
Alice tapped Pay on Bob’s row and typed 2: one Bob-coin left.
How the screens are taken. A scenario in the repository, glp_multiagent/test/apps_screens_test.dart,
runs every agent’s mini-app in one GLP engine, the agents’ conversations channels within it, and
takes each person’s acts by tapping and typing on the tabs’ controls; the test suite runs it headless
and checks every screen. On the simulator the same scenario runs in one app on an iPhone 16e,
one person’s phone on the screen at a time, and each screen is that phone’s screenshot.
The implementation as it stands. The compilation of Section 6 is implemented: the compiler
emits each program’s asking clauses, construct processes and dispatcher, the type checker checks
the result, and the test suite runs the programs of Sections 1 and 5 with scripted persons that
read the draws and grant on the constructs, each run checked against the committed sources. A
compiled module M is run by the goals
M # agent(MCh?, NetCh?, Asks), M # dispatch(Asks?, PersonCh?, MCh)
with the person channel PersonCh = ch(Grants?, Draws), its grants input(Id, P, R) —
the construct, the position and the person’s raw input — and its draws draw(Id, W, V) and
withdraw(Id) (Section 6.3). The Dart bridge, one construct family (Remark 6.4), draws these
constructs and returns the person’s input, and the apps above run through it. The super-app of [13],
as deployed on a physical smartphone, runs earlier programs of the three platforms, in an earlier
syntax of vGLP, compiled by an earlier compilation into an agent and a mediator; the four apps
above are compiled from their contracts.

Fig. 2. The apps of GKK, GSG, GSN and GC. Each is a screenshot of the GKK, GSG, GSN or GC mini-app,
compiled from that social contract down the abstraction cascade [16, 28, 53, 58, 59]. One instance of the
grassroots app [13] on an iPhone simulator runs the mini-app of every agent [13, 58]; the agents communicate
over channels within it [53], and the screenshot shows one person’s screen. All source code and all artefacts,
including the social contracts and the artefacts the abstraction cascade produces from them, are in the public
repository https://github.com/EShapiro2/GLP. GKK: (1) Alice before establishing her identity; (2) Bob asked to
come to know Alice; (3) Bob’s contacts after an introduction. GSG: (4) Bob asked to befriend Alice; (5) Alice’s
friends after the befriend; (6) after Bob ends the friendship. GSN: (7) Bob’s chat with Alice; (8) Bob asked
to join Alice’s group; (9) the group, with Bob’s message delivered; (10) Alice’s group. GC: (11) Alice minted;
(12) Bob asked to swap; (13) Bob’s holdings after the swap; (14) Alice paid.




9    RELATED WORK
vGLP makes two independent contributions: it conditions a machine step on a person’s volition,
and it derives the interface from the program’s semantics. The first relates to formal approaches
to user interface semantics, in which the person is a source of input, an environment, a chooser,
an editor, or a signer; we review these first, by the person’s role. The second relates to work that
derives the interface automatically, but from an application’s data, object model, or tasks rather
than from a semantics whose steps a person guards; and a further tradition predicts or verifies a
given interface. In none does a transaction wait on the wills of its guards, with an else-branch taken
when a guard does not will it, and in none is a running interface compiled from such transactions
with a proof that it implements them. Underlying these differences is what a human input is: in the
languages that present a program to its user it is a command driving a tool; in those that generate
an interface from data or tasks it is a value completing a task; in vGLP it is a volition authorising an
otherwise-ready machine transaction — a guard.
Volition Elicitation                                                                                 55


Formal approaches to user interface semantics. Functional reactive programming gave inter-
action a denotational semantics with Fran [14]; Fudgets composed graphical interfaces from stream
processors in a lazy functional language [8]; Elm gave a GUI language an operational semantics [10];
and Krishnaswami and Benton gave GUIs a denotational model with user input as nondeterminism,
by a monad [25]. Task-oriented programming executes typed editors the person fills: iTasks [40, 41],
and its formal core TopHat, whose labelled transitions consume user inputs [61]. iTasks derives
those editors generically from the task’s data types, and TopHat from the task’s syntax too, the
user continuing a step explicitly, so an interface is derived, from the data and the task, and the
inputs drive a workflow. TopHat proves that the inputs it computes for a task are exactly those its
semantics accepts [61], as Theorem 6.3 proves of a construct’s answers. In vGLP only the person
gives an answer (Theorem 5.14), the answers guard multiagent transactions, and the cascade is
proved correct. Game semantics models a program against an Opponent whose strategy is the
environment’s [23]; interaction trees represent impure programs whose events the environment
answers [64]; interactive computation makes the environment part of the computational model [62].
Formal human-computer interaction modelled interactive systems directly: the PIE model [11],
interactors [12], Petri-net interactive cooperative objects [36], statecharts [20], and the synchronous
languages [4]. These works define the semantics of an interface already designed. The concurrent
logic languages connected keyboard and screen as stream-processing agents [47]; a message to the
person could carry an unbound variable that the person’s response assigns. Ceptre’s interactive
stages let the player select which enabled linear-logic rule fires [30]: the person resolves nondeter-
minism. Hazelnut gives an operational semantics to the person’s edit actions on programs [34].
Smart-contract semantics fire a transition only on a transaction signed by a key [46]. Beliefs, desires,
and intentions in agent programming [42] are the software agent’s own, not a person’s.
The person in concurrency theory. In CSP [21], CCS [31] and the 𝜋-calculus [32] the environment
is a process, and a person is one more process or an external choice the environment resolves;
session types [22] discipline the conversation between processes and say nothing of who wills
it. The person’s role across the formal traditions — choice machines, I/O automata and reactive
systems, angelic and demonic nondeterminism, alternating-time logics, ceremony analysis — is
reviewed in [28], and GLP’s moded types are set against session types in [59]. Here the agent is
a person and a machine, and a machine transaction is subject to the person’s volition. A process
calculus would host the transition systems of Section 2 as well as GLP does; it does not by itself
derive an interface from a procedure’s interactive type.
The interface derived from the program. Deriving the interface from the program, rather
than authoring it separately, is an established idea, on bases distinct from ours. Naked objects
presents a system’s domain objects directly and creates the interface automatically from their
definitions by reflection [38, 39], in a noun-verb style: the person views objects and invokes their
methods, with no hand-written presentation layer. Model-based user-interface development derives
interfaces from separate task and domain models — the CAMELEON reference framework [5],
ConcurTaskTrees and model-based design [37], and description languages such as UIML [1] and
UsiXML [29], surveyed in [2]; task-oriented programming (above) derives its editors generically
from data types and tasks; and structure editors derive an editing interface from a grammar [44].
In each the interface follows from a description of the application’s data, objects, or tasks.
Typed widgets. Three works associate an interface element with a type, as vGLP associates a
widget with an interactive type (Section 6.2). Formlets [9] abstract a part of a web form that collects
a value of a given type: a formlet renders its HTML and parses the submission into a typed value,
formlets compose as idioms, and the handler attached to a form consumes the value. Tangible
values [15] pair a value with a visualizer whose structure follows the structure of the value’s type,
56                                                                           Idit Keidar and Ehud Shapiro


with a default input and output per type and alternatives chosen explicitly, and end users compose
them gesturally into new functions. Livelits [33] fill a typed hole in a program with a user-defined
GUI whose expansion is an expression of the hole’s type, kept live by evaluating the expressions
spliced into it in the closures collected around the hole. In each, the interface produces a value of a
type for a program or a programmer; in vGLP a widget forms a person’s answer to a question the
running program asks, and the reduction that reads the answer is the transaction the person wills.
Atomic commitment. Two-phase commit [19, 26] commits a transaction across sites on the votes
of all, a coordinator collecting the votes and announcing the outcome; Skeen’s decentralised form
has every site send its vote to every site and decide by itself, and his nonblocking protocol adds
a phase so that a site may decide under failures [60]; the textbooks [3, 18] treat the family. The
two-phase commit of Section 3.2 is the coordinated form, the agent that initiates the transaction
coordinating it, with a guard set in place of unanimous consent: the persons of the guard set whose
approval does not stand are asked, every other participant votes on its own precondition, and the
else-branch is taken when a person does not will the then-branch. Concurrent attempts are ordered
by wound-wait [45], a deadlock-prevention scheme for distributed databases: a busy participant
lets a start wait rather than refuse it, an attempt of higher priority makes the one of lower priority
that blocks it yield, and an attempt tried again after yielding keeps its priority, so that contention
neither deadlocks nor livelocks. Fault tolerance is left to work in preparation.
Predicting and verifying a given interface. Interface design is often treated as a craft outside
formal computer science, yet two lines formalise it. Predictive engineering models estimate a
design’s performance from the person’s elementary actions — the keystroke-level model and the
GOMS family [6, 7, 24]. Formal methods for interactive systems specify and verify interfaces —
from grammars for user interfaces [43] through the collected work of the field [35, 63], of which
the dialogue models above are part.
   To the best of our knowledge, vGLP is the first programming language whose operational
semantics conditions transactions on a person’s volition — elicited, awaited until given, and fulfilled
— with the interface derived from the questions that semantics asks, each rendered by the widget of
its interactive type, rather than from the application’s data or tasks.

10   CONCLUSION
We incorporated people in the operational semantics of a programming language: a goal of a vGLP
volitional procedure puts a typed question to its person, a clause that reads the answer reduces
only after the person has answered, and the person’s answers are their own. The structure of
the interface is derived from the semantics: a program’s questions are the interactive variables
of its goals, each rendered by the widget of its interactive type — a pair of yes and no buttons, a
form, the request form of a stream question, an input box for a stream. The abstraction cascade
proceeds from volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions, by two-phase commit coordinated
by the initiating agent, to communicating volitional agents, then by compilation to vGLP, and by
compilation onto GLP; it is proved correct down to GLP. The three platforms are compiled down
the cascade from their contracts and run on GLP with scripted persons, and the apps of the three,
so compiled, run on an iPhone simulator. A contract carries the words its app shows besides its
clauses and its schemas, so the app is compiled from the contract. The two-phase commit orders
contending attempts by wound-wait: every attempt ends, and contention keeps no transaction that
stays enabled from being carried out. An interactive type is the contract a user-interface developer
programs against: any construct family whose widgets preserve it inherits the theorems, so the
rendering of a platform is the person’s choice.
Volition Elicitation                                                                                                     57


   Future work: a widget language beyond the basic types, drawing on user-interface specification
languages; and a GLP channel per construct in the bridge, removing the dispatcher’s serial step.
